Formula <a href="#rot:product-path" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rot:product-path">[rot:product-path]</a> is constant across the plateau. The same disintegration with $\alpha=\beta$ gives the formula at non-atomic values, for almost every conditioning value.

For the algebraic claims, the Stieltjes identity $$d\widehat L=-s\,dl$$ follows from <a href="#rot:symbol" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rot:symbol">[rot:symbol]</a>. If $c$ denotes <a href="#rot:product-path" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rot:product-path">[rot:product-path]</a>, its jump at a common discontinuity $v$ is $$\Delta c=\widehat L_a(v-)\Delta p_b
                  +(\Delta p_a)\widehat L_b(v+).$$ Its continuous Stieltjes part obeys the corresponding product rule. Thus $d\widehat{L_a\star L_b}
=d(\widehat L_a\widehat L_b)$. At $1-$, subtracting the off-diagonal from the diagonal gives $(d_a-p_a(1-))(d_b-p_b(1-))$, the required endpoint product. The symbols consequently agree everywhere up to the choice of versions. Taking $s\downarrow0$ in <a href="#rot:product-path" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rot:product-path">[rot:product-path]</a> gives the root formula. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### Reconstruction in each spectral group

<a id="rot:f"></a>
<a id="rot:f-derivative"></a>
Let $b_t(x)$ be the inverse resolvent for the finite law $\sum_a\rho_a\delta_{t\lambda_a}$, and set $$\label{rot:f}
 f_a(x)=\frac{\rho_a}{b_t(x)-t\lambda_a}\quad(x>0),
 \qquad f_a(0)=0.$$ For a finite alphabet of positive proportions, $b_t(x)>\max_a t\lambda_a$ for $x>0$. The functions $f_a$ are smooth jointly in $x,t$, including $x=0$: near zero, apply the inverse function theorem with $1/b_t$ as the inverse variable. Moreover $$\label{rot:f-derivative}
 f_a'(x)=
 \frac{\rho_a/(b_t(x)-t\lambda_a)^2}
      {\sum_b\rho_b/(b_t(x)-t\lambda_b)^2},
 \qquad
 f_a'(0)=\rho_a,\qquad \sum_a f_a'(x)=1 .$$

<a id="stmt-rot-paths"></a>
<div id="rot:paths" class="proposition">

<a id="rot:full-paths"></a>
<a id="rot:function-path"></a>
**Proposition 18** (Projected-overlap paths). *Under the hypotheses of Proposition <a href="#rot:ward" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rot:ward">16</a>, let $p$ be the quantile of the total overlap. Then the entire group path is determined by $p$: $$\label{rot:full-paths}
 p_a(s)=\int_0^{p(s)}f_a'(D_p(r))\,dr,\qquad
 d_a=\int_0^1f_a'(D_p(r))\,dr .$$ These formulas hold at $t=0$, for repeated eigenvalues, and when $D_p(p(s))=0$. More generally, for a smooth scalar or matrix function $f$ on $[0,1]$, the path $$\label{rot:function-path}
 l_f(s)=\int_0^{p(s)}f'(D_p(r))\,dr,\qquad
 d_f=f(0)+\int_0^1f'(D_p(r))\,dr$$ has symbol $f(D_p(p(s)))$ and root $p(0+)f'(D_p(p(0+)))$. Symbol and root uniquely determine a bounded-variation path.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rot-paths"><summary>Proof of stmt-rot-paths</summary>

*Proof.* By Lemma <a href="#rot:product" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rot:product">17</a>, the two Ward identities combine into $$\rho_bL_a-\rho_aL_b
      =t(\lambda_a-\lambda_b)(L_a\star L_b).$$ Set $\alpha_a=\widehat L_a(s)$ and $x=\sum_a\alpha_a$. GG positivity and Gram bounds give $0\le p_a(s)\le d_a$, so $\alpha_a\ge0$, and $x=D_p(p(s))$. If $x>0$, every $\alpha_a$ is positive: a zero component paired with a positive component would give $-\rho_a\alpha_b=0$. Dividing the symbol equations therefore gives a common number $$\frac{\rho_a}{\alpha_a}+t\lambda_a=b
       \quad\hbox{for every }a.$$ The positive sum constraint is the resolvent equation for $b_t(x)$. Hence $\alpha_a=f_a(x)$. If $x=0$, all the symbols are zero, which is the same conclusion.

At the root, put $r_a=p_a(0+)$, $p_0=\sum_a r_a$, and $x=D_p(p_0)$. The root equations are $$\rho_b r_a-\rho_a r_b
 =t(\lambda_a-\lambda_b)
        \bigl(f_a(x)r_b+r_a f_b(x)\bigr).$$ For $x>0$, substitution of <a href="#rot:f" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rot:f">[rot:f]</a> shows that $$\frac{(b_t(x)-t\lambda_a)^2}{\rho_a}\,r_a$$ is independent of $a$. The sum constraint and <a href="#rot:f-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rot:f-derivative">[rot:f-derivative]</a> give $r_a=p_0f_a'(x)$. For $x=0$, the root equations directly give $\rho_b r_a=\rho_a r_b$, and therefore $r_a=\rho_a p_0=p_0 f_a'(0)$.

It remains to recover the paths, including their diagonals, from these data. Let $F_p(r)=|\{s:p(s)\le r\}|$. Then $D_p'(r)=-F_p(r)$ almost everywhere and $D_p(1)=0$. For the path in <a href="#rot:function-path" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rot:function-path">[rot:function-path]</a>, Fubini’s theorem gives $$\begin{split}
 \widehat L_f(s)
 &=f(0)+\int_{p(s)}^1 F_p(r)f'(D_p(r))\,dr\\
 &=f(D_p(p(s))).
 \end{split}$$ Since $D_p$ is constant on $[0,p_0]$, its root is $p_0 f'(D_p(p_0))$. Finally, equal symbols imply equal Stieltjes derivatives of the off-diagonal paths on every $[\epsilon,1)$, because $d\widehat L=-s\,dl$. The paths differ by a constant, fixed by the root, and the symbol then fixes the diagonal. This also treats jumps accumulating at zero. Applying this calculation to $f_a$ proves <a href="#rot:full-paths" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rot:full-paths">[rot:full-paths]</a>.

No step divided by an eigenvalue difference. At $t=0$, $f_a(x)=\rho_a x$, so $p_a=\rho_a p$ and $d_a=\rho_a$. Tied eigenvalues similarly give proportional paths in the tied groups. If $p\equiv1$, then $D_p\equiv0$ and $p_a=d_a=\rho_a$, also covered by the formulas. ◻

</details>

For later Gaussian marks, the diagonal excess in group $a$ is $$d_a-p_a(1-)=\int_{p(1-)}^1 f_a'(D_p(r))\,dr\ge0.$$ After division by $\rho_a$, the corresponding function in <a href="#rot:function-path" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rot:function-path">[rot:function-path]</a> is $H_a(x)=(b_t(x)-t\lambda_a)^{-1}$, with $H_a(0)=0$. Its derivative is bounded by $1/\rho_a$. Thus the group formulas give uniformly bounded Gaussian covariances for all trial paths, with zero residual variance when the top overlap is one.

<a id="sec:upper"></a>
### The upper comparison

We prove the upper bound in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> for a finite spectral law $\mu=\sum_{a=1}^m\rho_a\delta_{\lambda_a}$, with every $\rho_a>0$. The spectral counts may be any deterministic sequence whose proportions converge to these masses. The rotation identities express the limiting energy through the total-overlap quantile. We first identify an order in which this energy is monotone, then obtain that order by minimizing an enriched pressure against a fixed trial.

<a id="stmt-up-energy"></a>
<div id="up:energy" class="lemma">

<a id="up:tail-order"></a>
<a id="up:primitive"></a>
**Lemma 19** (Energy and tail order). *For $t\in[0,1]$ and $p\in\mathcal P$, define $$F_t(x)=xR(tx),\qquad
 \mathcal E_t(p)=\frac12\int_0^1F_t'(D_p(r))\,dr.$$ At a limit satisfying Proposition <a href="#rot:paths" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rot:paths">18</a>, $\mathcal E_t(p)$ is the expected interaction energy per spin with coefficient $\Lambda_N$, before multiplication by $t$. The function $F_t$ is convex on $[0,1]$. In particular, $$\label{up:tail-order}
 \int_s^1p(u)\,du\ge\int_s^1q(u)\,du
 \quad(0\le s\le1)
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \mathcal E_t(p)\le\mathcal E_t(q).$$ Moreover, $$\label{up:primitive}
 \int_0^t\mathcal E_u(q)\,du
 =G_t(q)=\frac12\int_0^1tR(tD_q(r))\,dr.$$ Writing $L=\max_a|\lambda_a|$, one has $\lvert\mathcal E_t(p)\rvert\le L/2$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-up-energy"><summary>Proof of stmt-up-energy</summary>

*Proof.* Let $b_t$ and $f_a$ be the resolvent functions for the spectrum $t\lambda_a$, as in Proposition <a href="#rot:paths" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rot:paths">18</a>. That proposition gives $$\frac12\sum_a\lambda_a d_a
 =\frac12\int_0^1\sum_a\lambda_a f_a'(D_p(r))\,dr.$$ For $t>0$, the identity $b_t(x)=t b(tx)$ implies $$\sum_a\lambda_a f_a(x)
 =\frac{x b_t(x)-1}{t}=xR(tx)=F_t(x).$$ The same identity holds by continuity at $t=0$ and at $x=0$. This proves the energy formula.

For $t>0$ and $x>0$, set $$M_j=\sum_a\frac{\rho_a}{(b_t(x)-t\lambda_a)^j}.$$ The resolvent equation says $M_1=x$, and differentiation yields $$b_t'(x)=-\frac1{M_2},\qquad
 b_t''(x)=\frac{2M_3}{M_2^3},\qquad
 (x b_t(x))''=\frac{2(M_1M_3-M_2^2)}{M_2^3}\ge0.$$ The inequality is Cauchy–Schwarz. Smooth extension at zero proves convexity on the closed interval; at $t=0$, $F_0$ is linear. Also $f_a'\ge0$ and $\sum_a f_a'=1$, so the displayed energy is an average of numbers in $[-L/2,L/2]$.

For a nondecreasing quantile, its positive-part integral can be recovered from upper tails: $$\int_0^1(p(u)-r)_+\,du
 =\max_{0\le s\le1}
   \left\{\int_s^1p(u)\,du-(1-s)r\right\}.$$ The hypothesis of <a href="#up:tail-order" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="up:tail-order">[up:tail-order]</a> therefore gives $D_p\le D_q$. Since $F_t'$ is nondecreasing, the energy order follows. Finally, $$\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\bigl[tR(tx)\bigr]
 =R(tx)+txR'(tx)=F_t'(x).$$ Integration in $t$ proves <a href="#up:primitive" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="up:primitive">[up:primitive]</a>, including its endpoint at zero. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-up-bound"></a>
<div id="up:bound" class="proposition">

**Proposition 20** (Finite-spectrum upper bound). *Suppose the deterministic eigenvalues belong to a fixed finite set $\{\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_m\}$, and their proportions converge to $\rho_a>0$. Then $$\limsup_{N\to\infty}\mathbb EP_N
 \le\inf_{p\in\mathcal P}\{S(p)+G_1(p)\}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-up-bound"><summary>Proof of stmt-up-bound</summary>

*Proof.* Fix a finite step trial $q$ with values strictly below one. Denote its interval weights by $w_i>0$, its values by $q_i$, and its left endpoints by $\zeta_i$, for $0\le i<k$. By Lemma <a href="#fld:domain" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fld:domain">13</a>, $S(q)<\infty$. Use the cascade on these intervals to form the enriched, perturbed pressure $P_N^{\mathrm{enr}}(t,h,\mathbf u,\mathbf v)$ of Section <a href="#sec:rotation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:rotation">4</a>. Its linear field has covariance levels $h_i$, and the exponent includes the subtraction $-Nh_{k-1}/2$.

Suppose that the upper bound $S(q)+G_1(q)$ fails along a subsequence. Choose $\delta>0$ small enough that, on this subsequence, the objective <a id="up:objective"></a>$$\label{up:objective}
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathcal J_N(t,h,\mathbf u,\mathbf v)
 ={}&-\mathbb EP_N^{\mathrm{enr}}(t,h,\mathbf u,\mathbf v)
  -\frac12\int_0^1q(s)h(s)\,ds\\
 &+S(q)+G_t(q)+t\delta+\Pi_N(\mathbf u,\mathbf v).
 \end{aligned}$$ has value at most $-\eta$, for some fixed $\eta>0$, at $t=1$, $h=0$, and all perturbation parameters equal to $3/2$. Here $$\Pi_N(\mathbf u,\mathbf v)
 =\sum_{j\le N}2^{-j}(u_j-3/2)^2
  +\sum_a(v_a-3/2)^2.$$ This follows from the $O(e_N)$ perturbation comparison. Minimize $\mathcal J_N$ on $$0\le t\le1,\qquad
 0\le h_0\le\cdots\le h_{k-1}\le H,\qquad
 \mathbf u\in[1,2]^N,\quad\mathbf v\in[1,2]^m,$$ where the fixed cap $H$ will be chosen below. Gaussian and direct comparison make the objective continuous, so a minimum exists.

<a id="up:zero-time"></a>
At a negative minimum, increasing the field in nonnegative monotone directions will force the limiting overlap quantile to have upper tails at least as large as those of $q$. Lemma <a href="#up:energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="up:energy">19</a> will then order their energies. A feasible decrease in $t$ gives the opposite energy inequality, with the strict margin $\delta$. To make these two variations available, we first exclude $t=0$ and the field cap. At $t=0$, the unperturbed enriched pressure is exactly $\ell(h)$. The definition of $S(q)$ and the uniform perturbation bound give $$\label{up:zero-time}
 \mathcal J_N(0,h,\mathbf u,\mathbf v)\ge-Ce_N.$$ In fact the minimizing values of $t$ stay away from zero. For fixed other parameters, Lemma <a href="#up:energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="up:energy">19</a> and the spin norm bound give $$\left|\partial_t\mathcal J_N\right|\le L+\delta.$$ Comparing a minimum of value at most $-\eta$ with <a href="#up:zero-time" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="up:zero-time">[up:zero-time]</a>, while retaining its other parameters, yields $t_N\ge\eta/[2(L+\delta)]$ for all sufficiently large $N$.

<a id="up:cap-estimate"></a>
For the field cap, the recursion and $\log\cosh z\le|z|$ give $$\label{up:cap-estimate}
 \ell(h)\le
 -\frac12\sum_iw_i h_i+\sqrt{\frac{2h_0}{\pi}}+C_q.$$ Indeed a Gaussian increment of variance $v$ at positive exponent $\zeta_i$ sends $|z|$ to a function bounded by $|z|+\zeta_i v/2+(\log2)/\zeta_i$. Iterate over the positive levels and take ordinary expectation at the root. The identity $\sum_i\zeta_i(h_i-h_{i-1})=h_{k-1}-\sum_iw_i h_i$ then proves <a href="#up:cap-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="up:cap-estimate">[up:cap-estimate]</a>; when $k=1$, the sum of positive-level constants is empty.

The interaction changes the normalized log partition by at most $L/2$, so $$\mathbb EP_N^{\mathrm{enr}}(t,h,\mathbf u,\mathbf v)
 \le\ell(h)+L/2+Ce_N.$$ Put $\epsilon=1-\max_iq_i>0$. Since $S(q)\ge0$, $G_t(q)\ge-L/2$, and the penalty is nonnegative, <a href="#up:cap-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="up:cap-estimate">[up:cap-estimate]</a> implies, on $h_{k-1}=H$, $$\mathcal J_N
 \ge\frac{\epsilon w_{k-1}}2H
       -\sqrt{\frac{2H}{\pi}}-C_q-L-Ce_N.$$ Choose $H$ large enough to make this positive for large $N$. Thus every minimum under consideration has $h_{k-1}<H$. The partition of the trial is fixed throughout this choice; no uniformity in its number of intervals is required.

At these minima, Proposition <a href="#rot:gg" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rot:gg">15</a> applies to the deterministic minimizing perturbation parameters. Pass to a subsequence so that $t$ and the overlap arrays converge, and let $p$ be the limiting quantile of $Q_{12}$. The resulting array satisfies joint GG for the projected overlaps and the cascade level $T$. The cascade marking identity preserves the level probabilities $w_i$. Proposition <a href="#arr:structure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="arr:structure">4</a> therefore represents $Q_{12}$ and $T_{12}$ by nondecreasing quantiles on the same unit interval. The quantile $p$ need not be constant on the trial intervals.

We now use the field coordinates of the minimum to order the upper tails of $p$ and $q$. Let $g$ be any nonnegative nondecreasing step function on the fixed trial intervals, with values $g_i$, and define $\gamma_g(i/k)=g_i$ on the cascade levels. A small increase of $h$ in direction $g$ is feasible, even if some levels are zero or tied, because the upper cap is inactive. The covariance derivative of the linear field is $NQ_{12}\gamma_g(T_{12})$. Its diagonal term is canceled by the subtraction $-Nh_{k-1}/2$, so minimality gives $$0\le D_g\mathcal J_N
 =\frac12\mathbb E\langle Q_{12}\gamma_g(T_{12})\rangle
  -\frac12\int_0^1q(s)g(s)\,ds.$$ By synchronized quantiles, the limit of the first expectation is $\int_0^1p(s)g(s)\,ds$. Hence <a id="up:contact-order"></a>$$\label{up:contact-order}
 \int_0^1p(s)g(s)\,ds\ge\int_0^1q(s)g(s)\,ds.$$

Choose $g$ to be each upper-tail indicator of the partition. This proves the tail inequalities at its endpoints. The function $s\mapsto\int_s^1p$ is concave, because $p$ is nondecreasing, and therefore lies above its chords. The corresponding tail integral of $q$ is affine on each trial interval. The endpoint inequalities consequently hold at every $s\in[0,1]$. Lemma <a href="#up:energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="up:energy">19</a> now yields $\mathcal E_t(p)\le\mathcal E_t(q)$.

It remains to vary $t$ at the same minimum. A move to the left is feasible, including when $t_N=1$, so $\partial_t\mathcal J_N\le0$. Proposition <a href="#rot:paths" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rot:paths">18</a> and Lemma <a href="#up:energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="up:energy">19</a> identify the limit of this derivative as $$-\mathcal E_t(p)+\mathcal E_t(q)+\delta\ge\delta>0.$$ This contradiction proves the upper bound for the fixed step trial $q$.

Finally, given any $p\in\mathcal P$ with $S(p)<\infty$, round its values down to finite steps $q_n$ such that $q_n\le p$, $\max q_n<1$, and $\|q_n-p\|_\infty\to0$. Nonnegativity of every admissible field gives $S(q_n)\le S(p)$, while $$\|D_{q_n}-D_p\|_\infty\le\|q_n-p\|_1$$ and continuity of $R$ give $G_1(q_n)\to G_1(p)$. The step-trial bounds therefore imply $$\limsup_{N\to\infty}\mathbb EP_N\le S(p)+G_1(p).$$ Trials with infinite $S(p)$ impose no further restriction, and taking the infimum proves the proposition. ◻

</details>

The field supremum remains inside the trial infimum: the contact argument fixes $q$ before introducing its finite-dimensional minimum. Section <a href="#sec:cavity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:cavity">6</a> supplies the matching lower bound, and Section <a href="#sec:general" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:general">7</a> passes from finite alphabets to the spectral laws of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.

<a id="sec:cavity"></a>
### The cavity lower bound

The cavity comparison belongs to the variational approach of Aizenman, Sims, and Starr <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[ASS2003](#ref-ASS2003)]. Here the reservoir and added coordinates are coupled through fresh directions in the spectral eigenspaces; the rotation and quadratic-tilt identities required by this coupling are derived below.

We now prove the lower bound for a finite spectral alphabet with positive rational proportions. We compare systems of sizes $N$ and $N+n$, where $n$ is fixed and $s_a:=n\rho_a$ is integral for every $a$. The comparison must retain the base interaction: its eigenspaces contain the fresh directions that become Gaussian cavity variables. After evaluating the resulting change of measure, site symmetry will identify its field as a maximizer in the definition of $S$.

<a id="cav:counts"></a>
Fix a progression of dimensions with step $n$ and group counts satisfying $$\label{cav:counts}
 k_a(N+n)=k_a(N)+s_a,
 \qquad \frac{k_a(N)}{N}\longrightarrow\rho_a.$$ All limits and uniform bounds in this section concern sufficiently large dimensions on this progression, with the alphabet, its proportions, and $n$ fixed. We use the unenriched perturbations of Section <a href="#sec:rotation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:rotation">4</a> at $t=1$. Write $\theta=(u,v)$ for their parameters, $\eta$ for the product uniform probability on their parameter space, and $$Z_N^\theta=\text{the perturbed partition function},\qquad
 \mathcal F_N(\theta)=\mathbb E\log Z_N^\theta.$$ The cube prior in $Z_N^\theta$ remains its uniform probability. Proposition <a href="#rot:gg" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rot:gg">15</a> supplies measurable sets $\mathcal G_N$ with $\eta(\mathcal G_N)\to1$ such that every sequence $\theta_N\in\mathcal G_N$ has, after extraction, joint GG limits with fixed spectral diagonals. We first bound the increments at any such sequence, and average over parameters only at the end.

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### A coupling that preserves the base law

Let $E:\mathbb R^n\to\mathbb R^{N+n}$ embed the last $n$ coordinates, and let $P_a^+$ be the full spectral projectors. Define $$M_a=E^{\mathsf T}P_a^+E,\qquad
 d=(m-1)n.$$ The matrices $M_a$ converge in probability to $\rho_a I_n$, and are positive definite almost surely for all sufficiently large dimensions. The first assertion follows from rotational symmetry and concentration; the second follows, for example, by representing a uniform frame with independent Gaussians. Throughout, zero-dimensional complementary spaces have the usual empty-matrix conventions.

<a id="stmt-cav-coupling"></a>
<div id="cav:coupling" class="lemma">

<a id="cav:blocks"></a>
<a id="cav:factor"></a>
<a id="cav:base-moments"></a>
**Lemma 21** (Coupled interaction and fresh frames). *There is a coupling of the full interaction of size $N+n$ and an interaction of size $N$ with the spectral counts in <a href="#cav:counts" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:counts">[cav:counts]</a> with the following properties. There are $d$ orthonormal special axes in the first $N$ coordinates and matrices $$\label{cav:blocks}
 \begin{pmatrix}A_N&L_N\\L_N^{\mathsf T}&C_N\end{pmatrix}
 \longrightarrow
 \begin{pmatrix}A&L\\L^{\mathsf T}&C\end{pmatrix}
 \quad\text{in probability},
 \qquad C=\Big(\sum_a\rho_a\lambda_a\Big)I_n,$$ where $A_N,A$ act on $\mathbb R^d$. Fix a diagonal matrix $A_0$ with $n-s_a$ copies of $\lambda_a$. If $y\in\mathbb R^d$ is the projection of a base spin $\sigma$ onto the special axes, then adjoining $\varepsilon\in\{-1,1\}^n$ changes the main exponent by $$\label{cav:factor}
 \log W_N(y,\varepsilon)
 =\frac12y^{\mathsf T}(A_N-A_0)y
   +y^{\mathsf T}L_N\varepsilon
   +\frac12\varepsilon^{\mathsf T}C_N\varepsilon.$$ Conditional on the base spectral projectors, the special axes assigned to group $a$ form a uniform orthonormal $(n-s_a)$-frame in that group. They may be taken independent of the base perturbations given those projectors. For finitely many base replicas, their projection variables converge jointly with any overlap limit to centered Gaussian marks, independent across the axes conditional on the overlap array, with covariance $A^a_{lj}/\rho_a$ for an axis assigned to group $a$. Moreover, for every fixed $q>0$, $$\label{cav:base-moments}
 \sup_{N,\theta}\mathbb E\langle |y|^q\rangle_{N,\theta}<\infty.$$ Here the brackets denote the base Gibbs probability and the expectation also includes the special frames.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cav-coupling"><summary>Proof of stmt-cav-coupling</summary>

*Proof.* The mutually orthogonal spaces $\operatorname{ran}(P_a^+E)$ have orthonormal coordinate maps $P_a^+E M_a^{-1/2}$. In their direct sum, the cavity coordinate map is the vertical stack $(M_a^{1/2})_a$. Its columns are orthonormal because $\sum_aM_a=I_n$. Complete these columns to an orthonormal basis, with the complement first, using a rule continuous near the limiting stack $(\sqrt{\rho_a}I_n)_a$. One may project a fixed limiting complementary frame and orthonormalize it. In these coordinates the interaction is the left matrix of <a href="#cav:blocks" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:blocks">[cav:blocks]</a>. Its limit is a conjugate of $\bigoplus_a\lambda_a I_n$, with cavity column blocks $\sqrt{\rho_a}I_n$. This proves <a href="#cav:blocks" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:blocks">[cav:blocks]</a>.

The complement to the cavity coordinates in this direct sum lies in the first $N$ coordinates and has dimension $d$; its chosen basis gives the special axes. All other eigenvectors also lie in the first $N$ coordinates, with multiplicities $k_a(N+n)-n$. Retain them and assign $A_0$ to the special axes. The resulting base multiplicity is <a id="cav:decomposition"></a>$$k_a(N+n)-n+(n-s_a)=k_a(N+n)-s_a=k_a(N).$$ The geometric decomposition is thus $$\label{cav:decomposition}
 \mathbb R^{N+n}
 =%
  \BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,ActualText=2A01005F00610020211B005F00610020002800640069006D0020003D0020004E00202212002000640029}%
  \underbrace{\bigoplus_a\mathcal R_a}_{\dim=N-d}%
  \EndAccSupp{}%

   \mathbin{\oplus}
   %
  \BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,ActualText=D835DCB1005F004E0020002800640069006D0020003D002000640029}%
  \underbrace{\mathcal V_N}_{\dim=d}%
  \EndAccSupp{}%

   \mathbin{\oplus}
   %
  \BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,ActualText=0045211D005E006E0020002800640069006D0020003D0020006E0029}%
  \underbrace{E\mathbb R^n}_{\dim=n}%
  \EndAccSupp{}%
,
 \qquad
 \mathcal R_a=\operatorname{ran}P_a^+\cap\ker E^{\mathsf T}.$$ The base interaction agrees with the full interaction on the first summand and uses $A_0$ on $\mathcal V_N$. Subtracting the two quadratic forms gives <a href="#cav:factor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:factor">[cav:factor]</a>.

Conditional on $(M_a)_a$, rotations of the first $N$ coordinates act transitively on the remaining frame and subspace data. Their conditional law is therefore the invariant probability on this orbit. In particular the base interaction has its Haar law, independently of $(M_a)_a$. After conditioning further on the base projectors, the remaining freedom is the product of the rotations within their eigenspaces. The selected axes consequently have the asserted uniform frame law. Generate the base perturbations independently conditional on the projectors, using their prescribed Gaussian kernels. This preserves that conditional frame law; no full-system Gaussian coefficient realization is inherited by the base.

For a uniform unit vector in a group of dimension $k_a(N)$, its projections on fixed replicas have covariance $$\frac{(\sigma^l)^{\mathsf T}P_a\sigma^j}{k_a(N)}
 =\frac{N}{k_a(N)}A^a_{lj}.$$ Generate the fixed number of selected vectors by independent Gaussian vectors divided by $\sqrt{k_a(N)}$, then orthonormalize them. Their Gram matrix tends to the identity. Before orthonormalization the replica projections are already jointly Gaussian with the displayed covariances, which are uniformly bounded. The Gram correction thus does not change the limit. The sphere moment formula, or Gaussian radial independence, gives every fixed moment uniformly, since $\|P_a\sigma\|\le\sqrt N$ and $k_a(N)/N$ stays bounded below. This proves <a href="#cav:base-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:base-moments">[cav:base-moments]</a> for any base Gibbs probability that is independent of the selected frames conditional on the projectors. ◻

</details>

Figure <a href="#fig:cavity-decomposition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:cavity-decomposition">1</a> summarizes the shared spectral spaces and the two interactions on the special coordinates.

<figure id="fig:cavity-decomposition">

<figcaption>The coupled cavity decomposition, with schematic block widths. The full and base interactions agree on the remaining spectral spaces $ℛ_{a}$. The span of the $m$ spectral images of the cavity coordinates splits into the special complement and the cavity itself; the full interaction on this span has each eigenvalue $λ_{a}$ with multiplicity $n$. Assigning $A_{0}$ to the special complement gives the prescribed base multiplicities. When $m = 1$, the special complement is absent. Conditional on the base spectral projectors, the $n − s_{a}$ special axes assigned to group $a$ form a uniform orthonormal frame. The base perturbations are generated independently of these frames conditional on those projectors.</figcaption>
</figure>

Visual witness description of PDF page 29 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
A block diagram illustrating matrix/space decompositions with columns corresponding to dimensions and subspaces, and rows corresponding to "Full" and "Base" systems.
- At the top spanning the right two columns: an overarching bracket labeled $\bigoplus_a \operatorname{ran}(P_a^+ E)$ and "$m\text{ spectral subspaces of dimension } n$".
- Three column header boxes below:
  1. Left column: $\bigoplus_a R_a$, "dimension $N - d$".
  2. Middle column: "special complement $V_N$", "$d = (m - 1)n$".
  3. Right column: "cavity $E\mathbb{R}^n$", "dimension $n$".
- Top row (labeled "Full"):
  - Left box: "$\lambda_a\text{ on } R_a$", "$\dim R_a = k_a(N + n) - n$".
  - Followed by a direct sum symbol $\oplus$.
  - A $2 \times 2$ block matrix partitioned into four blocks: top-left $A_N$, top-right $L_N$, bottom-left $L_N^T$, bottom-right $C_N$.
- Text between the Full and Base rows below the left column box: "retained without change".
- Bottom row (labeled "Base"):
  - Left box: "$\lambda_a\text{ on } R_a$".
  - Followed by a direct sum symbol $\oplus$.
  - Middle box: "$A_0 = \bigoplus_a \lambda_a I_{n - s_a}$".
  - Right box: "adjoined spins", "$\varepsilon \in \{-1, 1\}^n$".
```


<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### Perturbations and bounded Gibbs tests

The logarithmic comparison will give a lower bound involving one field in the supremum defining $S$. The cavity-spin tests will show that this field is maximizing.

In averages containing $\varepsilon$, use its independent uniform probability on the cavity cube. Write $\langle\cdot\rangle_0$ for this probability times the perturbed base Gibbs probability. All expectations below include the coupled spectral data and the special frames. Let $W$ denote <a href="#cav:factor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:factor">[cav:factor]</a> with the limiting matrices.

<a id="stmt-cav-comparison"></a>
<div id="cav:comparison" class="lemma">

<a id="cav:increment-comparison"></a>
**Lemma 22** (Finite-size comparison). *There are deterministic $\delta_N\downarrow0$, uniform over the perturbation parameters, such that $$\label{cav:increment-comparison}
 \mathcal F_{N+n}(\theta)-\mathcal F_N(\theta)
 \ge
 \mathbb E\log\big\langle
 W_N(y,\varepsilon)e^{-\delta_N(1+|y|^2)}
 \big\rangle_0.$$ The right-hand side is bounded below uniformly in $N$ and $\theta$. In addition, any bounded continuous test of the two-replica total overlap and the cavity spins in the full system can be evaluated, up to an error that vanishes first as $N\to\infty$ and then as $B\to\infty$, using the base probability reweighted by $W_N\mathbf 1_{\{|y|\le B\}}$ and the base total overlap.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cav-comparison"><summary>Proof of stmt-cav-comparison</summary>

*Proof.* We condition first on all coupled spectral data. Outside the special/cavity coordinates the full and base projectors coincide. Their overlap difference, including the change of dimension divisor, is bounded on two configurations by <a id="cav:overlap-error"></a>$$\label{cav:overlap-error}
 \frac{C_n}{N}\big(1+|y^1|^2+|y^2|^2\big).$$ Multiplying by $Ne_N$ for the deterministic perturbations or by $Ne_N^2$ for their Gaussian covariances gives a vanishing coefficient. The changes in these amplitudes for a bounded jump of dimensions also tend to zero. The omitted Gaussian kernels and their derivatives remain summable with the geometric weights. Thus the deterministic perturbation difference is bounded by $o(1)(1+|y|^2)$, and the covariance difference by $o(1)(1+|y^1|^2+|y^2|^2)$, uniformly in the parameters.

To see the sign of the comparison, put $g(\sigma)=1+|y|^2$. Let $D_0,D_+$ be the deterministic base and full perturbations on $(\sigma,\varepsilon)$, and let $\mathcal C_0,\mathcal C_+$ be the corresponding Gaussian perturbation covariance kernels, conditional on the coupled spectral data. The preceding estimates give deterministic $a_N,b_N\to0$, uniform in the parameters and spectral data, such that $$|D_+-D_0|\le a_Ng,\qquad
 |(\mathcal C_+-\mathcal C_0)(1,2)|
       \le b_N(g(\sigma^1)+g(\sigma^2)).$$ Choose independent centered Gaussian fields $X_0,X_+$ with these conditional covariances. Keep the full main interaction fixed and add the interpolating perturbation $$(1-\tau)D_0+\tau D_+
 +\sqrt{1-\tau}\,X_0+\sqrt\tau\,X_+
 -(1-\tau)\delta_Ng,\qquad 0\le\tau\le1.$$ Write $Z_\tau$ and $\langle\cdot\rangle_\tau^{\mathrm{int}}$ for its partition function and Gibbs probability. The diagonal and pair terms in Lemma <a href="#arr:gaussian-calculus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="arr:gaussian-calculus">8</a> are each bounded below by $-b_N\mathbb E\langle g\rangle_\tau^{\mathrm{int}}$. Consequently $$\frac{d}{d\tau}\mathbb E\log Z_\tau
 \ge(\delta_N-a_N-2b_N)\mathbb E\langle g\rangle_\tau^{\mathrm{int}}\ge0$$ if $\delta_N\downarrow0$ is chosen to dominate $a_N+2b_N$. At $\tau=0$ the partition function is $Z_N^\theta\langle W_Ne^{-\delta_Ng}\rangle_0$; at $\tau=1$ it is $Z_{N+n}^\theta$. Integrating proves <a href="#cav:increment-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:increment-comparison">[cav:increment-comparison]</a>. The block matrices are uniformly bounded, so Jensen’s inequality bounds its right-hand side below by $-C\mathbb E\langle1+|y|^2\rangle_0$. Lemma <a href="#cav:coupling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cav:coupling">21</a> gives the claimed uniform lower bound.

<a id="cav:full-tail"></a>
For the test comparison, choose $c>0$ smaller than every $\rho_a$. The event $\min_a\lambda_{\min}(M_a)<c$ has probability tending to zero and can be discarded in bounded tests. On its complement, the orthonormal coordinates of the projection onto $\bigoplus_a\operatorname{ran}(P_a^+E)$ give $$|y|^2+n
 =\sum_a\big|M_a^{-1/2}E^{\mathsf T}P_a^+\sigma^+\big|^2
 \le c^{-1}\sum_a|E^{\mathsf T}P_a^+\sigma^+|^2,
 \qquad \sigma^+=(\sigma,\varepsilon).$$ At each fixed parameter choice the full disorder law is invariant under site permutations. For each site $j$, $$\mathbb E\langle(P_a^+\sigma^+)_j^2\rangle_+
 =\frac1{N+n}\mathbb E\langle\|P_a^+\sigma^+\|^2\rangle_+
 \le1.$$ Consequently $$\label{cav:full-tail}
 \limsup_{N\to\infty}\mathbb E\langle\mathbf 1_{\{|y|>B\}}\rangle_+
 \le\frac{C}{B^2}.$$ This use of symmetry precedes the restriction; no symmetry of the restricted Gibbs probability is needed. Nor is an inverse moment of $M_a$ required on the discarded event.

For fixed $B$, the restriction $|y|\le B$ is a fixed set once the spectral data have been conditioned on. It is nonempty for $B^2>d$, since uniform base-spin sampling has mean squared projection $d$. The perturbation differences on that set are bounded by $O((a_N+b_N)(1+B^2))$. Use the same interpolation without its penalty, normalizing the Gibbs probability on this fixed restriction at every $\tau$. Lemma <a href="#arr:gaussian-calculus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="arr:gaussian-calculus">8</a> bounds the change in the expected bounded Gibbs test by $O((a_N+b_N)(1+B^2))=o(1)$ for fixed $B$. At the base endpoint its probability is precisely the base probability tilted by $W_N\mathbf 1_{\{|y|\le B\}}$. Finally, $|Q_{12}^+-Q_{12}|\le2n/(N+n)$, so uniform continuity permits replacing the full total overlap by the base one. Equation <a href="#cav:full-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:full-tail">[cav:full-tail]</a> then removes the restriction in the stated order. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### Gaussian limits with explicit cutoffs

The finite-size comparison has not asserted convergence of an unbounded exponential factor. We now pass to overlap limits with cutoffs in place. The subsequent quadratic calculation will supply the uniform estimates needed to remove those cutoffs.

<a id="cav:path-convention"></a>
Take $\theta_N\in\mathcal G_N$ and a subsequence with limiting total overlap quantile $p$. By Proposition <a href="#rot:paths" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rot:paths">18</a>, the Gaussian marks supplied by Lemma <a href="#cav:coupling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cav:coupling">21</a> have the covariance path associated with <a id="cav:H"></a>$$\label{cav:H}
 H(x)=(b(x)I_d-A_0)^{-1},\qquad H(0)=0:$$ their off-diagonal covariance at quantile coordinate $s$ and their diagonal covariance are, respectively, $$\label{cav:path-convention}
 \int_0^{p(s)}H'(D_p(r))\,dr,
 \qquad
 \int_0^1H'(D_p(r))\,dr.$$ Indeed each diagonal entry of $H$ is $f_a/\rho_a$ for its assigned group. The derivative $H'$ is positive semidefinite, so these paths have nonnegative covariance increments and a nonnegative diagonal excess. If the top overlap equals one, that excess is zero.

Both the logarithmic increment and the cavity-spin tests must pass to these Gaussian marks. We keep separate cutoffs for the two quantities until we have controlled the marks under the full cavity reweighting.

Choose finite-valued nondecreasing maps converging uniformly to the identity on $[0,1]$, and let $\bar p$ be the resulting value roundings of $p$. The rounded overlap arrays are exactly the finite cascades of Lemma <a href="#arr:rpc-gg" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="arr:rpc-gg">6</a>. On each such cascade use Gaussian marks with <a href="#cav:path-convention" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:path-convention">[cav:path-convention]</a> recalculated with $\bar p$. The functions $H,H'$ are smooth on $[0,1]$ for the present finite alphabet, and $D_{\bar p}\to D_p$ uniformly. The recalculated covariances therefore converge uniformly to the required ones. Finite-replica Gaussian laws converge, including all the overlap labels, by Lemma <a href="#arr:finite-replica" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="arr:finite-replica">7</a>.

For the logarithmic lower bound, cap the factor in <a href="#cav:increment-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:increment-comparison">[cav:increment-comparison]</a> above at $e^T$, with $T>0$ fixed, and first replace the logarithm of its integral by the logarithm of that integral plus a floor $\epsilon>0$. This is a bounded continuous function of a bounded integral. Polynomial approximation and extra replicas give its limit as $N\to\infty$, then its finite-cascade approximation as $\bar p\to p$. The vanishing penalty and <a href="#cav:blocks" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:blocks">[cav:blocks]</a> do not change that limit: they converge uniformly on compact sets of $y$, whose complements have vanishing probability by <a href="#cav:base-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:base-moments">[cav:base-moments]</a>.

<a id="cav:negative-log"></a>
The floor can now be removed uniformly. If $Z_{N,T}$ is the capped base integral, Jensen’s inequality and the bounded block matrices give $$\label{cav:negative-log}
 (\log Z_{N,T})_-
 \le C\langle1+|y|^2\rangle_0,
 \qquad
 \sup_N\mathbb E[(\log Z_{N,T})_-^2]<\infty.$$ The same bound holds for the limiting Gaussian calculations and all finite-cascade approximations. Splitting according as $-\log Z_{N,T}$ is larger than a fixed number, and then increasing that number, proves that adding the floor changes the expected logarithm by a quantity tending uniformly to zero as $\epsilon\downarrow0$.

<a id="cav:normalizer"></a>
For the restricted tests, fix $B>0$ and let $q_{N,B}=\langle\mathbf 1_{\{|y|>B\}}\rangle_0$. The base moment bound and Markov’s inequality give $$\label{cav:normalizer}
 \mathbb P(q_{N,B}>1/2)\le\frac{C}{B^2},
 \qquad
 \big\langle W_N\mathbf 1_{\{|y|\le B\}}\big\rangle_0
 \ge\frac12e^{-C(1+B^2)}
 \quad\text{if }q_{N,B}\le1/2.$$ The same estimates hold in the finite cascades. Thus denominator powers in a bounded replica test may first be regularized, with an arbitrarily small error apart from $C/B^2$, and then approximated by polynomials using extra replicas. The sphere $|y|=B>0$ has zero probability for a centered Gaussian law, even when its covariance is singular. This justifies the restricted finite-replica passage.

The order is now explicit. At fixed cap or radius and positive regularizer, take the base-system limit and then refine the finite rounding. Remove the regularizer using <a href="#cav:negative-log" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:negative-log">[cav:negative-log]</a> or <a href="#cav:normalizer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:normalizer">[cav:normalizer]</a>. It remains to remove the cap and the radius in the finite-cascade calculations with errors uniform in the rounding. This last step requires the actual quadratic transformation, to which we turn next.

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### Quadratic Gaussian reweighting

The quadratic part of the cavity factor changes the covariance of its Gaussian marks. The following lemma computes that change while keeping track of the expected logarithm of the normalizing factor. In particular, the matrices in the quadratic factor need not commute.

<a id="stmt-cav-quadratic"></a>
<div id="cav:quadratic" class="lemma">

**Lemma 23** (Quadratic tilt of a Gaussian cascade). *Let $\mu=\sum_{a=1}^m\rho_a\delta_{\lambda_a}$, where $\rho_a>0$ and $\sum_a\rho_a=1$, and put $\lambda_+=\max_a\lambda_a$. For $0<x\leq1$, let $b(x)>\lambda_+$ be the solution of $\sum_a\rho_a/(b(x)-\lambda_a)=x$. Let $A_0,A$ be symmetric $d\times d$ matrices whose eigenvalues are at most $\lambda_+$, and define <a id="cav:q-def"></a>$$\label{cav:q-def}
 K=A-A_0,\qquad H(x)=(b(x)\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}-A_0)^{-1}\quad(x>0),\qquad H(0)=0.$$ Let $p\in\mathcal P$ be constant with value $p_i$ on intervals of lengths $w_i>0$, for $0\leq i<k$, where $0\leq p_0\leq\cdots\leq p_{k-1}\leq1$ and $\sum_iw_i=1$. Set $\zeta_i=\sum_{j<i}w_j$, so that $\zeta_0=0$ and $\zeta_k=1$.*

<a id="cav:q-path"></a>
<a id="cav:q-log"></a>
*On the corresponding Ruelle probability cascade, construct the marks from centered Gaussian increments independent of one another and of the unmarked cascade, with off-diagonal covariance path and diagonal covariance $$\label{cav:q-path}
 Q_H(s)=\int_0^{p(s)}H'(D_p(r))\,dr,
 \qquad V_H=\int_0^1H'(D_p(r))\,dr.$$ Here the covariance shared by two sampled paths whose last common level is $i$ is $Q_H(s)$ for $s$ in interval $i$. Any diagonal excess is an independent Gaussian residual, integrated separately at each visit to a leaf. More explicitly, write $v_\alpha$ for the cascade weights and $y_\alpha$ for the sum of the common and branching Gaussian increments. If $\gamma_\Sigma$ denotes the centered Gaussian probability measure with covariance $\Sigma$, set $$Z_K=\sum_\alpha v_\alpha
       \int\exp\left(\frac12(y_\alpha+z)^{\mathsf T}
                              K(y_\alpha+z)\right)
              \gamma_{V_H-Q_H(1-)}(dz).$$ Then $Z_K$ is positive and finite almost surely, its logarithm is integrable, and $$\label{cav:q-log}
 \mathbb E\log Z_K
 =-\frac12\int_0^1
   \left.\frac{d}{dx}\log\det(\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}-H(x)K)\right|_{x=D_p(r)}\,dr.$$*

<a id="cav:q-transform"></a>
*After normalizing this quadratic tilt and relabeling the tree, the cascade weights have their original law. The terminal marks can be represented by independent Gaussian increments, independent of the unmarked cascade, with the covariance convention <a href="#cav:q-path" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:q-path">[cav:q-path]</a> using $$\label{cav:q-transform}
 \widetilde H(x)=(\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}-H(x)K)^{-1}H(x)
               =(b(x)\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}-A)^{-1}\quad(x>0),
 \qquad \widetilde H(0)=0$$ in place of $H$. This representation includes the tilted residual draws and hence applies to joint sampling of any finite number of terminal marks. The derivatives and integrands at zero in these formulas mean their smooth limits. For $d=0$, determinants equal one and all Gaussian marks are the unique vector in $\mathbb R^0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cav-quadratic"><summary>Proof of stmt-cav-quadratic</summary>

*Proof.* We first verify the conditional Gaussian integrals needed by the cascade change of measure. We then compute the normalizing constants and the covariances under its tilted kernels. The case $d=0$ is immediate, so assume $d\geq1$.

The finite alphabet makes $b(x)$ well defined above $\lambda_+$. Writing $u=1/b$ near $x=0$ changes its defining equation to $x=\sum_a\rho_a u/(1-\lambda_a u)$, whose derivative in $u$ at zero is one. Consequently $H$ and $\widetilde H$ extend smoothly to zero, with $H'(0)=\widetilde H'(0)=\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}$. For positive $x$, put $M_2(x)=\sum_a\rho_a/(b(x)-\lambda_a)^2$. Differentiation gives $b'(x)=-1/M_2(x)$ and $H'(x)=M_2(x)^{-1}(b(x)\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}-A_0)^{-2}\succeq0$. In particular, all covariance increments in <a href="#cav:q-path" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:q-path">[cav:q-path]</a> are well defined and positive semidefinite.

<a id="cav:q-increments"></a>
Append $p_k=1$ and put $p_{-1}=0$. For $0\leq i\leq k$, define $$\label{cav:q-increments}
 \Sigma_i=\int_{p_{i-1}}^{p_i}H'(D_p(r))\,dr,
 \qquad H_i=H(D_p(p_i)),\qquad
 J_i=(\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}-H_iK)^{-1},\qquad K_i=KJ_i.$$ The inverse defining $J_i$ exists: at positive arguments, $H_i^{-1}-K=b(D_p(p_i))\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}-A\succ0$, and at zero $H_i=0$. Also $K_i=(\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}-KH_i)^{-1}K$ is symmetric. The increment $\Sigma_0$ is common to all leaves, increments $\Sigma_i$ for $1\leq i<k$ are attached independently to the edges at level $i$, and $\Sigma_k$ is the residual. Since $D_p$ has slope $-\zeta_i$ on $(p_{i-1},p_i)$ for $i>0$, while it is constant on $(0,p_0)$, we have <a id="cav:q-levels"></a>$$\label{cav:q-levels}
 H_k=0,\qquad
 H_{i-1}-H_i=\zeta_i\Sigma_i\quad(1\leq i\leq k),
 \qquad
 \Sigma_0=p_0H'(D_p(p_0)).$$ These identities also hold when some adjacent overlap values coincide.

<a id="cav:q-factor"></a>
<a id="cav:q-precision"></a>
For $i>0$, write $\Delta_i=\zeta_i\Sigma_i$. The matrix ordering in the following identity will be important: $$\label{cav:q-factor}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Id}}-\Delta_iK_i=(\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}-H_{i-1}K)J_i,
 \qquad
 \det(\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}-\Delta_iK_i)
   =\frac{\det(\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}-H_{i-1}K)}{\det(\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}-H_iK)}.$$ The symmetric Gaussian precision matrix satisfies $$\label{cav:q-precision}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Id}}-\zeta_i\Sigma_i^{1/2}K_i\Sigma_i^{1/2}\succ0.$$ To prove this, replace $\Delta_i$ in <a href="#cav:q-factor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:q-factor">[cav:q-factor]</a> by $t\Delta_i$, $0\leq t\leq1$, and set $S_t=H_i+t\Delta_i$. If $H_{i-1}=0$, then $\Sigma_i=0$ and the claim is immediate. Otherwise $H_{i-1}\succ0$, and each nonzero $S_t$ is positive definite and at most $H_{i-1}$. Thus $$S_t^{-1}-K\succeq H_{i-1}^{-1}-K\succ0.$$ It follows that $\det(\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}-S_tK)\ne0$; this is also true when $S_t=0$. By <a href="#cav:q-factor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:q-factor">[cav:q-factor]</a> and the identity $\det(\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}-BC)=\det(\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}-CB)$, the symmetric matrix in <a href="#cav:q-precision" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:q-precision">[cav:q-precision]</a>, with $\zeta_i$ replaced by $t\zeta_i$, never has a zero eigenvalue. At $t=0$ it is the identity, proving <a href="#cav:q-precision" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:q-precision">[cav:q-precision]</a> by continuity.

<a id="cav:q-gaussian-integral"></a>
For a vector $u\in\mathbb R^d$, completion of the square now gives the finite integral $$\label{cav:q-gaussian-integral}
 \frac1{\zeta_i}\log\int
       \exp\left(\frac{\zeta_i}{2}
                    (u+z)^{\mathsf T}K_i(u+z)\right)
                    \gamma_{\Sigma_i}(dz)
 =-\frac1{2\zeta_i}\log\det(\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}-\Delta_iK_i)
       +\frac12u^{\mathsf T}K_{i-1}u.$$ Indeed, if $T_i=(\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}-\Delta_iK_i)^{-1}$, then <a href="#cav:q-factor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:q-factor">[cav:q-factor]</a> implies $J_iT_i=J_{i-1}$ and hence $K_iT_i=K_{i-1}$. Formula <a href="#cav:q-gaussian-integral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:q-gaussian-integral">[cav:q-gaussian-integral]</a> also holds for singular $\Sigma_i$, by writing $z=\Sigma_i^{1/2}g$ with $g$ a standard Gaussian vector.

<a id="cav:q-constants"></a>
Starting at $K_k=K$, backward integration therefore produces the quadratic coefficients $K_i$ of <a href="#cav:q-increments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:q-increments">[cav:q-increments]</a>. For $1\leq i<k$, <a href="#cav:q-gaussian-integral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:q-gaussian-integral">[cav:q-gaussian-integral]</a> verifies exactly the conditional fractional exponential moment of exponent $\zeta_i$ required by the marked-cascade change of measure in Proposition <a href="#arr:rpc" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="arr:rpc">5</a>. The residual step $i=k$ uses exponent $\zeta_k=1$ and verifies its ordinary exponential moment. The root uses exponent zero, namely ordinary expectation of the backward value; its quadratic contribution is $\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(\Sigma_0K_0)/2$. Since a Gaussian quadratic polynomial is integrable, no exponential moment of the root quadratic term is needed. The same recursion and Proposition <a href="#arr:rpc" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="arr:rpc">5</a>, conditional on the root mark, show that $Z_K$ is positive and finite almost surely. Their logarithmic integrability assertion applies because the Gaussian integrals above have finite constants and the root value is a quadratic polynomial in a finite-dimensional Gaussian vector. Thus $$\label{cav:q-constants}
 \mathbb E\log Z_K
 =\frac12\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(\Sigma_0K_0)
 -\sum_{i=1}^k\frac1{2\zeta_i}
   \log\frac{\det(\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}-H_{i-1}K)}{\det(\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}-H_iK)}.$$ Set $f(x)=\log\det(\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}-H(x)K)$. Its derivative is $f'(x)=-\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(H'(x)K(\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}-H(x)K)^{-1})$. On $(0,p_0)$ this identifies the root term in <a href="#cav:q-constants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:q-constants">[cav:q-constants]</a> with $-\frac12\int_0^{p_0}f'(D_p(r))\,dr$. On every subsequent interval, the change of variable $D_p'(r)=-\zeta_i$ identifies its determinant term with the corresponding integral. Summing proves <a href="#cav:q-log" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:q-log">[cav:q-log]</a>.

It remains to identify the terminal marks under the quadratic tilt. The marked-cascade rule leaves the root law unchanged and gives, at step $i>0$, the Gaussian kernel obtained by multiplying the density of $z\sim\gamma_{\Sigma_i}$ by $\exp(\zeta_i(u+z)^{\mathsf T}K_i(u+z)/2)$ and normalizing. If $Y_{i-1}=u$ is the running sum before this step, square completion gives <a id="cav:q-kernels"></a>$$\label{cav:q-kernels}
 \mathbb E[Y_i\mid Y_{i-1}]=T_iY_{i-1},\qquad
 \mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}(Y_i\mid Y_{i-1})=T_i\Sigma_i,
 \qquad T_i=(\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}-\Delta_iK_i)^{-1}.$$ The covariance is symmetric and positive semidefinite, since it equals $$\Sigma_i^{1/2}
 (\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}-\zeta_i\Sigma_i^{1/2}K_i\Sigma_i^{1/2})^{-1}
 \Sigma_i^{1/2}.$$ The conditional covariance is independent of the preceding value, and the kernels on distinct child edges are conditionally independent.

<a id="cav:q-innovations"></a>
<a id="cav:q-resolvent-difference"></a>
Rescale the running sums by $U_i=J_iY_i$. Because $J_iT_i=J_{i-1}$, the process $U_i$ has centered independent innovations on the tree. Their covariances, and that of the common root, are respectively $$\label{cav:q-innovations}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}(U_i-U_{i-1})=J_{i-1}\Sigma_iJ_i^{\mathsf T}\quad(i>0),
 \qquad \mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}(U_0)=J_0\Sigma_0J_0^{\mathsf T}.$$ For symmetric matrices $B,C$ for which the indicated inverses exist, the map $F(B)=(\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}-BK)^{-1}B$ satisfies $$\label{cav:q-resolvent-difference}
 F(B)-F(C)
 =(\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}-BK)^{-1}(B-C)(\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}-KC)^{-1}.$$ This follows by multiplying on the left by $\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}-BK$ and on the right by $\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}-KC$. Applying it to $B=H_{i-1}$ and $C=H_i$ in <a href="#cav:q-innovations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:q-innovations">[cav:q-innovations]</a> shows that the covariance at step $i>0$ is $$\frac{\widetilde H(D_p(p_{i-1}))-
       \widetilde H(D_p(p_i))}{\zeta_i}
 =\int_{p_{i-1}}^{p_i}\widetilde H'(D_p(r))\,dr.$$ Here <a href="#cav:q-transform" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:q-transform">[cav:q-transform]</a> follows directly from $H(x)^{-1}-K=b(x)\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}-A$ at positive $x$. Differentiating <a href="#cav:q-resolvent-difference" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:q-resolvent-difference">[cav:q-resolvent-difference]</a> along $H(x)$ also gives <a id="cav:q-root-covariance"></a>$$\label{cav:q-root-covariance}
 \widetilde H'(x)=J(x)H'(x)J(x)^{\mathsf T},\qquad
 J(x)=(\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}-H(x)K)^{-1},$$ so the root covariance in <a href="#cav:q-innovations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:q-innovations">[cav:q-innovations]</a> is $p_0\widetilde H'(D_p(p_0))$, as required. Since $J_k=\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}$, the final sum $U_k$ equals the original terminal mark $Y_k$. The cascade change of measure gives the original unmarked cascade law independently of these Gaussian innovations, completing the claimed representation.

All the endpoint cases are included. If $p_{k-1}=1$, then $\Sigma_k=0$ and the residual step makes no change. If $p=1$ everywhere, then $D_p=0$, $J_i=\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}$, and $\Sigma_0=\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}$; the sole quadratic contribution is the ordinary root expectation $\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(K)/2$. Equal adjacent overlap values simply give zero increments in the same formulas. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### Removing cutoffs and evaluating the increment

Apply Lemma <a href="#cav:quadratic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cav:quadratic">23</a> to the matrices supplied by Lemma <a href="#cav:coupling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cav:coupling">21</a>. The eigenvalues of $A$ and $A_0$ are at most $\lambda_+$: $A$ is a compression of $\bigoplus_a\lambda_a I_n$, and $A_0$ has the specified spectral alphabet. The lemma therefore applies to every finite rounding $\bar p$. Its joint Gaussian representation will bound the two-replica moment arising from the remaining linear tilt. That bound removes both cutoffs uniformly in the rounding, after which we compute the increment and the spin tests.

For a finite step quantile $q$, denote the cascade prior with the Gaussian covariance convention <a href="#cav:path-convention" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:path-convention">[cav:path-convention]</a>, including the independent residual and uniform cavity spins, by $\langle\cdot\rangle_q$. Write $\langle\cdot\rangle_{q,W}$ for its normalized tilt by $W(y,\varepsilon)$.

<a id="stmt-cav-cutoffs"></a>
<div id="cav:cutoffs" class="lemma">

<a id="cav:tilted-moments"></a>
**Lemma 24** (Uniform removal of cutoffs). *For the fixed finite alphabet and fixed $n$, and every $r>0$, $$\label{cav:tilted-moments}
 \sup_{q\ \mathrm{finite\ step}}
 \mathbb E\langle |y|^r\rangle_{q,W}<\infty.$$ Consequently the error in the expected logarithm from replacing $W$ by $\min(W,e^T)$ tends uniformly to zero as $T\to\infty$. The error in a bounded replica test from restricting to $|y|\le B$ tends uniformly to zero as $B\to\infty$, with any fixed bounded convention if the restricted normalizer is zero.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cav-cutoffs"><summary>Proof of stmt-cav-cutoffs</summary>

*Proof.* First perform only the quadratic tilt. By Lemma <a href="#cav:quadratic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cav:quadratic">23</a>, the terminal marks then have a Gaussian cascade representation associated with $\widetilde H(x)=(b(x)I_d-A)^{-1}$. Its diagonal covariance is $$\int_0^1\widetilde H'(D_q(r))\,dr,$$ whose norm is bounded by $\sup_{0\le x\le1}\|\widetilde H'(x)\|$. This is finite for the fixed alphabet, independently of the number of steps and their weights. Indeed $b(x)>\lambda_+$ for $x>0$, and the inverse has the smooth extension at zero already proved in the lemma.

Let $\langle\cdot\rangle_{q,K}$ denote the normalized quadratic tilt, still including the uniform cavity-spin probability. The remaining exponent is $$V(y,\varepsilon)=y^{\mathsf T}L\varepsilon
                  +\frac12\varepsilon^{\mathsf T}C\varepsilon.$$ For each realization, Jensen’s inequality gives $\langle e^V\rangle_{q,K}^{-1}\le\langle e^{-V}\rangle_{q,K}$. Taking two replicas therefore gives <a id="cav:linear-moment-bound"></a>$$\label{cav:linear-moment-bound}
 \mathbb E\langle|y|^r\rangle_{q,W}
 \le\mathbb E\big\langle |y^1|^r e^{V(y^1,\varepsilon^1)
                                      -V(y^2,\varepsilon^2)}
                \big\rangle_{q,K}.$$ Conditional on their branching, the two marks on the right are centered joint Gaussians with uniformly bounded covariance. The spin coefficients range over a fixed finite set. Gaussian polynomial-exponential moments thus bound the right side uniformly, proving <a href="#cav:tilted-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:tilted-moments">[cav:tilted-moments]</a>. This argument includes the residual draws as part of the probability prior. Jensen’s inequality and the same exponential-linear moments also show that the remaining logarithmic normalizer is integrable.

Put $g_T=(\log W-T)_+$. Since $\min(W,e^T)=We^{-g_T}$, $$0\le\log\langle W\rangle_q
       -\log\langle\min(W,e^T)\rangle_q
 \le\langle g_T\rangle_{q,W}.$$ The bound $|\log W|\le C(1+|y|^2)$ and <a href="#cav:tilted-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:tilted-moments">[cav:tilted-moments]</a> make its expectation tend uniformly to zero. Restriction of an $l$-replica bounded test changes its expectation by at most a constant times $l\langle\mathbf 1_{\{|y|>B\}}\rangle_{q,W}$. Markov’s inequality and <a href="#cav:tilted-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:tilted-moments">[cav:tilted-moments]</a> prove the second assertion, including the event where the restricted normalizer is zero. ◻

</details>

For $p\in\mathcal P$, define <a id="cav:field-path"></a>$$\label{cav:field-path}
 h_p(s)=\int_0^{p(s)}R'(D_p(r))\,dr.$$ This is a bounded nonnegative nondecreasing path. For $x>0$, $$R'(x)=\frac1{x^2}-\frac1{M_2(x)}\ge0,
 \qquad M_2(x)=\sum_a\frac{\rho_a}{(b(x)-\lambda_a)^2}\ge x^2,$$ and the derivative has its smooth nonnegative limit at zero. If $p$ is a finite step quantile, then $h_p\in\mathcal H$.

<a id="stmt-cav-evaluation"></a>
<div id="cav:evaluation" class="proposition">

<a id="cav:evaluated"></a>
<a id="cav:spin-test"></a>
**Proposition 25** (Evaluation of the cavity factor). *For every finite step quantile $p$, the cascade factor constructed above satisfies $$\label{cav:evaluated}
 \frac1n\mathbb E\log\langle W\rangle_p
 =\ell(h_p)+\frac12\int_0^1p(s)h_p(s)\,ds+G_1(p).$$ For a fixed cavity site $j$ and a bounded continuous function $\phi$, the same tilted calculation satisfies $$\label{cav:spin-test}
 \mathbb E\langle\phi(Q_{12})\varepsilon_j^1\varepsilon_j^2\rangle_{p,W}
 =\int_0^1\phi(p(s))B_{h_p}(s)\,ds,$$ where $Q_{12}$ is the common-level value prescribed by $p$ and $B_{h_p}$ is the conditional two-spin product from Proposition <a href="#fld:derivative" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fld:derivative">9</a>. The common-level distribution itself is unchanged by the tilt.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cav-evaluation"><summary>Proof of stmt-cav-evaluation</summary>

<a id="cav:scalar-field"></a>
<a id="cav:determinant"></a>
<a id="cav:determinant-derivative"></a>
*Proof.* Compression of the resolvent of the limiting block matrix onto the cavity coordinates gives, for $b=b(x)$, <a id="cav:compression"></a>$$\label{cav:compression}
 \big(bI_n-C-L^{\mathsf T}(bI_d-A)^{-1}L\big)^{-1}
 =\sum_a\frac{\rho_a I_n}{b-\lambda_a}=xI_n.$$ The whole block has eigenvalue $\lambda_a$ with multiplicity $n$. Its Schur determinant and the multiplicities in $A_0$ give $$\begin{aligned}
 C+L^{\mathsf T}\widetilde H(x)L&=R(x)I_n,
       \label{cav:scalar-field}\\
 \log\det(I_d-H(x)K)
 &=\log\frac{\det(bI_d-A)}{\det(bI_d-A_0)}\notag\\
 &=n\left(\log x+\sum_a\rho_a\log(b-\lambda_a)\right).
       \label{cav:determinant}
\end{aligned}$$ To see the multiplicities explicitly, $\det(bI_d-A)=x^n\prod_a(b-\lambda_a)^n$ whereas $\det(bI_d-A_0)=\prod_a(b-\lambda_a)^{n-s_a}$. Differentiating <a href="#cav:determinant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:determinant">[cav:determinant]</a> and using $\sum_a\rho_a/(b-\lambda_a)=x$ yields $$\label{cav:determinant-derivative}
 \frac1n\frac{d}{dx}\log\det(I_d-H(x)K)
 =\frac1x+xb'(x)=xR'(x).$$

<a id="cav:before-parts"></a>
Differentiating <a href="#cav:scalar-field" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:scalar-field">[cav:scalar-field]</a> gives $$L^{\mathsf T}\widetilde H'(x)L=R'(x)I_n.$$ Thus, after the quadratic transformation, the field $L^{\mathsf T}y$ has independent coordinates with common-level covariance path $h_p$. Its diagonal covariance is $vI_n$, where $$v=\int_0^1R'(D_p(r))\,dr.$$ Writing $h_*=h_p(1-)$, the independent residual has covariance $(v-h_*)I_n$. Since every cavity spin has squared norm $n$, integrating this residual and the deterministic cavity term $C=R(0)I_n$ contributes $n[R(0)+v-h_*]/2$ to the logarithm. The remaining spin sum is $\prod_{j=1}^n\cosh z_j$ for the nonresidual field $z$. Its logarithm is additive over coordinates, so the Gaussian backward recursion and its tilted kernels factor over those coordinates. This recursion contributes $n[\ell(h_p)+h_*/2]$. Lemma <a href="#cav:quadratic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cav:quadratic">23</a> and <a href="#cav:determinant-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:determinant-derivative">[cav:determinant-derivative]</a> therefore give $$\label{cav:before-parts}
 \frac1n\mathbb E\log\langle W\rangle_p
 =\ell(h_p)+\frac12\left[
 R(0)+\int_0^1(1-D_p(r))R'(D_p(r))\,dr\right].$$ All three contributions include the root interval; the independent residual was integrated at exponent one.

Fubini’s theorem and the definition of $D_p$ give $$\int_0^1p(s)h_p(s)\,ds
 =\int_0^1R'(D_p(r))\int_{\{s:p(s)>r\}}p(s)\,ds\,dr,$$ and, almost everywhere in $r$, $$1-D_p(r)-\int_{\{s:p(s)>r\}}p(s)\,ds=-rD_p'(r).$$ Subtracting the pairing with $h_p$ from the bracket in <a href="#cav:before-parts" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:before-parts">[cav:before-parts]</a> thus gives $$R(0)-\int_0^1r\,dR(D_p(r))
 =\int_0^1R(D_p(r))\,dr=2G_1(p),$$ where $D_p(1)=0$ supplies the endpoint at one. This proves <a href="#cav:evaluated" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:evaluated">[cav:evaluated]</a>.

Both the quadratic transformation and the remaining spin tilt use the marked-cascade rule of Proposition <a href="#arr:rpc" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="arr:rpc">5</a>. Its tree relabeling preserves all common-level values, and leaves their law unchanged. The scalar residual correction is independent of the spins. Conditional on a common level, the remaining single-coordinate two-spin product is exactly $B_{h_p}$ from the Ising recursion; the independent coordinates factor through its tilted kernels. This proves <a href="#cav:spin-test" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:spin-test">[cav:spin-test]</a> and the last assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="cav:preliminary-lower"></a>
We can now finish the passage from finite size to the evaluated increment. For a good parameter sequence with limiting quantile $p$, the capped comparison, finite-replica approximation, and Lemma <a href="#cav:cutoffs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cav:cutoffs">24</a> imply $$\label{cav:preliminary-lower}
 \liminf_{N\to\infty}
 \frac{\mathcal F_{N+n}(\theta_N)-\mathcal F_N(\theta_N)}{n}
 \ge\ell(h_p)+\frac12\int_0^1p(s)h_p(s)\,ds+G_1(p).$$ Here $\ell(h_p)$ uses the bounded-path extension of Theorem <a href="#fld:concavity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fld:concavity">12</a>. Indeed uniform rounding gives $h_{\bar p}\to h_p$ uniformly, so the right side of <a href="#cav:evaluated" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:evaluated">[cav:evaluated]</a> converges by the $L^1$ continuity of $\ell$. At each fixed logarithmic cap the limiting lower bound is the capped finite-cascade value; its difference from the uncapped value tends uniformly to zero by Lemma <a href="#cav:cutoffs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cav:cutoffs">24</a>. This verifies <a href="#cav:preliminary-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:preliminary-lower">[cav:preliminary-lower]</a> in the cutoff order already specified, without untruncated moment convergence at finite system size.

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
#### The maximizing field and the lower bound

The expression in <a href="#cav:preliminary-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:preliminary-lower">[cav:preliminary-lower]</a> contains one field, whereas the proposed answer contains a supremum over fields. To obtain the required inequality, we must prove that this particular field maximizes that supremum. The information comes from cavity-spin tests, not from the logarithmic comparison alone.

<a id="stmt-cav-self-consistency"></a>
<div id="cav:self-consistency" class="proposition">

<a id="cav:B-limit"></a>
<a id="cav:supremum"></a>
**Proposition 26** (Cavity self-consistency). *Let $p$ be a limiting total-overlap quantile along any sequence $\theta_N\in\mathcal G_N$ used in <a href="#cav:preliminary-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:preliminary-lower">[cav:preliminary-lower]</a>. For value roundings $\bar p\to p$ as above, $$\label{cav:B-limit}
 B_{h_{\bar p}}\longrightarrow p\quad\text{in }L^1(0,1).$$ Consequently $$\label{cav:supremum}
 S(p)=\ell(h_p)+\frac12\int_0^1p(s)h_p(s)\,ds.$$ Thus $h_p$ attains the field supremum in the bounded-path completion.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cav-self-consistency"><summary>Proof of stmt-cav-self-consistency</summary>

<a id="cav:test-identity"></a>
*Proof.* For any fixed cavity site $j$, site permutation symmetry of the full model gives, for every bounded continuous $\phi$, $$\mathbb E\langle\phi(Q_{12}^+)\varepsilon_j^1\varepsilon_j^2\rangle_+
 =\mathbb E\langle\phi(Q_{12}^+)Q_{12}^+\rangle_+.$$ Apply the restricted test comparison of Lemma <a href="#cav:comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cav:comparison">22</a>, pass through the bounded finite-replica calculations, and then remove the restriction using Lemma <a href="#cav:cutoffs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cav:cutoffs">24</a>. Proposition <a href="#cav:evaluation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cav:evaluation">25</a> and preservation of the common-level law give $$\label{cav:test-identity}
 \lim_{\bar p\to p}\int_0^1
 \phi(\bar p(s))\big(B_{h_{\bar p}}(s)-\bar p(s)\big)\,ds=0.$$ No GG property of the full system was used here: the base parameters are good, while site symmetry holds for the full model at every fixed parameter choice.

The functions $B_{h_{\bar p}}$ are nondecreasing and take values in $[0,1]$, so they are relatively compact in $L^1$. We explain why any subsequential limit $B$ is a measurable function of $p$; this is needed to infer equality from <a href="#cav:test-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:test-identity">[cav:test-identity]</a>. Since each rounding is a function of the values of $p$, it is constant on every plateau of $p$. The corresponding field levels are tied there. Their zero increments give identical conditional positions and magnetizations, so $B_{h_{\bar p}}$ is also constant there, by Proposition <a href="#fld:derivative" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fld:derivative">9</a>. An $L^1$ limit preserves this property on every positive-length plateau. There are only countably many such intervals. Outside their interiors and their null set of endpoints, the generalized inverse of the nondecreasing function $p$ recovers the quantile coordinate. Thus $B$ is measurable with respect to $p$ almost everywhere, including when $p$ has a singular-continuous part.

Passing to the limit in <a href="#cav:test-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:test-identity">[cav:test-identity]</a> shows that the signed pushforward of $(B-p)\,ds$ under $p$ vanishes, since continuous tests determine finite measures on $[0,1]$. The established measurability then gives $B=p$ almost everywhere. Every subsequential limit is the same, proving <a href="#cav:B-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:B-limit">[cav:B-limit]</a>.

For any fixed $v\in\mathcal H$, take common refinements of its intervals and those of $h_{\bar p}$. The supporting inequality from Theorem <a href="#fld:concavity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fld:concavity">12</a>, including tied levels, is $$\ell(v)\le\ell(h_{\bar p})
 -\frac12\int_0^1B_{h_{\bar p}}(s)
                    (v(s)-h_{\bar p}(s))\,ds.$$ Letting $\bar p\to p$ proves that the supremum defining $S(p)$ is at most the right side of <a href="#cav:supremum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:supremum">[cav:supremum]</a>. Conversely, $h_{\bar p}\in\mathcal H$, and their values in the definition of $S(p)$, with $p$ fixed in the pairing, converge to that same expression. This proves <a href="#cav:supremum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:supremum">[cav:supremum]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-cav-lower"></a>
<div id="cav:lower" class="proposition">

**Proposition 27** (Finite-alphabet lower bound). *Let $\mu=\sum_{a=1}^m\rho_a\delta_{\lambda_a}$ have positive rational proportions. Fix $n$ with $n\rho_a\in\mathbb N$ for every $a$, and a progression of dimensions and spectral counts satisfying <a href="#cav:counts" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:counts">[cav:counts]</a>. Along this progression, the unperturbed pressure satisfies $$\liminf_{N\to\infty}\mathbb EP_N
 \ge\inf_{p\in\mathcal P}\{S(p)+G_1(p)\}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cav-lower"><summary>Proof of stmt-cav-lower</summary>

*Proof.* Put $\mathcal V=\inf_{p\in\mathcal P}\{S(p)+G_1(p)\}$. This is finite: $S\ge0$, $G_1$ is bounded, and the zero trial has finite $S$ by Lemma <a href="#fld:domain" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fld:domain">13</a>. Every sequence of good base parameters has a subsequence with the overlap limits used above. Equations <a href="#cav:preliminary-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:preliminary-lower">[cav:preliminary-lower]</a> and <a href="#cav:supremum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cav:supremum">[cav:supremum]</a> therefore imply $$\liminf_{N\to\infty}\inf_{\theta\in\mathcal G_N}
 \big(\mathcal F_{N+n}(\theta)-\mathcal F_N(\theta)\big)
 \ge n\mathcal V.$$ Indeed any failure would select a sequence of parameters whose subsequential overlap limit contradicts the two equations.

Lemma <a href="#cav:comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cav:comparison">22</a> gives a uniform lower bound for the increments also outside $\mathcal G_N$. Since $\eta(\mathcal G_N)\to1$, integration over the common parameter space yields $$\liminf_{N\to\infty}\int
 \big(\mathcal F_{N+n}(\theta)-\mathcal F_N(\theta)\big)\,\eta(d\theta)
 \ge n\mathcal V.$$ The parameter space includes the whole infinite sequence, with the size-$N$ partition function ignoring later coordinates. Hence these integrated increments telescope exactly along the progression. Dividing their sum by the terminal dimension proves the lower bound for the parameter-averaged perturbed pressure. The perturbations change the expected log partition by $O(Ne_N)=o(N)$, uniformly in parameters, so the same bound holds for $\mathbb EP_N$. ◻

</details>

The fixed-$n$ comparison is now complete. Its constants may depend on the finite alphabet and on $n$; no uniformity across later spectral approximations was used. Section <a href="#sec:general" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:general">7</a> will combine this bound with the upper bound and remove the rational-count and finite-alphabet restrictions.

<a id="sec:general"></a>
### General spectral distributions

The upper and lower bounds now determine the pressure for finite spectral alphabets with rational proportions along the progressions used in the cavity argument. We first remove these arithmetic restrictions, and then approximate an arbitrary compactly supported spectral law. In this section, subscripts in $R_\nu$ and $G_{t,\nu}$ specify the spectral law; $\mathcal F(\nu)$ denotes the variational value in <a href="#eq:variational" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:variational">[eq:variational]</a> with that law. The functions $S$ and $D_p$ do not depend on the spectral law.

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
#### Continuity of the spectral term

The transform can reach the upper support edge, so its continuity must include that branch of the definition. The following statement needs no regularity of the measure near the edge.

<a id="stmt-gen-r-continuity"></a>
<div id="gen:R-continuity" class="lemma">

<a id="gen:R-uniform"></a>
<a id="gen:variational-continuity"></a>
**Lemma 28**. *Let $\nu_j$ and $\nu$ be probability measures supported in a common interval $[-K,K]$. Suppose that $\nu_j$ converges weakly to $\nu$ and that $$e_j:=\max\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\nu_j\longrightarrow e:=\max\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\nu.$$ Define $b_{\nu_j}$ and $R_{\nu_j}$ by the edge convention of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, and likewise for $\nu$. Then $$\label{gen:R-uniform}
 \sup_{0\le x\le1}|R_{\nu_j}(x)-R_\nu(x)|\longrightarrow0.$$ Consequently, $$\label{gen:variational-continuity}
 |\mathcal F(\nu_j)-\mathcal F(\nu)|
 \le \frac12\sup_{0\le x\le1}|R_{\nu_j}(x)-R_\nu(x)|
 \longrightarrow0.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-gen-r-continuity"><summary>Proof of stmt-gen-r-continuity</summary>

<a id="gen:b-bounds"></a>
*Proof.* For a measure $\tau$ with upper support edge $e_\tau$, put $$H_\tau(z)=\int\frac{\tau(d\lambda)}{z-\lambda},
 \qquad z>e_\tau.$$ This function is continuous and strictly decreasing. Thus $b_\tau(x)>e_\tau$ exactly when $x<H_\tau(e_\tau+)$, and in that case $H_\tau(b_\tau(x))=x$. In the remaining case $b_\tau(x)=e_\tau$. In both cases, $$\label{gen:b-bounds}
 e_\tau\le b_\tau(x)\le e_\tau+x^{-1},
 \qquad -K\le R_\tau(x)\le K.$$ The first upper bound follows from $H_\tau(z)\le(z-e_\tau)^{-1}$. The lower bound on $R_\tau$ follows from $H_\tau(z)\ge(z+K)^{-1}$, using the edge limit of this inequality when the transform is capped.

We first work away from zero. Let $x_j\to x>0$ and pass to a subsequence on which $b_{\nu_j}(x_j)\to b_*$. The bounds <a href="#gen:b-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="gen:b-bounds">[gen:b-bounds]</a> imply that such subsequences exist and that $b_*\ge e$. If $b_*>e$, the defining equations are uncapped for large $j$. Weak convergence, with their arguments uniformly separated from the supports, gives $$H_\nu(b_*)=x.$$ Hence $b_*=b_\nu(x)$. If $b_*=e$ but $b_\nu(x)>e$, choose $c\in(e,b_\nu(x))$. Then $H_\nu(c)>x$, so $H_{\nu_j}(c)>x_j$ for large $j$. Strict monotonicity, including the definition of the capped branch, forces $b_{\nu_j}(x_j)>c$, a contradiction. These two cases prove $$b_{\nu_j}(x_j)\longrightarrow b_\nu(x).$$ The same argument with the measure fixed proves continuity of $b_\nu$ on $(0,1]$. A compactness argument now gives uniform convergence of $R_{\nu_j}$ on every $[\varepsilon,1]$.

<a id="gen:R-at-zero"></a>
Near zero the convergence is uniform for a different reason. If $K=0$ there is nothing to prove. For $K>0$ and $x<(4K)^{-1}$, the transform cannot be capped, since $H_\tau(e_\tau+)\ge(2K)^{-1}$. Write $b_\tau(x)=x^{-1}+R_\tau(x)$ in its defining equation. The bound $|R_\tau|\le K$ permits the uniform expansion $$1=\int\frac{\tau(d\lambda)}{1+x(R_\tau(x)-\lambda)}
  =1-x\left(R_\tau(x)-\int\lambda\,\tau(d\lambda)\right)
    +O(K^2x^2).$$ Therefore $$\label{gen:R-at-zero}
 R_\tau(x)=\int\lambda\,\tau(d\lambda)+O(K^2x),$$ with a constant independent of $\tau$. Weak convergence on the common bounded interval implies convergence of the means. Combining <a href="#gen:R-at-zero" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="gen:R-at-zero">[gen:R-at-zero]</a> with the convergence away from zero proves <a href="#gen:R-uniform" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="gen:R-uniform">[gen:R-uniform]</a>.

Finally, $0\le D_p(r)\le1$ for every admissible $p$ and $r$, and hence $$\sup_{p\in\mathcal P}|G_{1,\nu_j}(p)-G_{1,\nu}(p)|
 \le\frac12\|R_{\nu_j}-R_\nu\|_\infty.$$ Taking infima after adding the common functional $S$ proves <a href="#gen:variational-continuity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="gen:variational-continuity">[gen:variational-continuity]</a>. These infima are finite: $S\ge0$ and $S(0)=0$ by Lemma <a href="#fld:domain" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fld:domain">13</a>, whereas the transforms are bounded by <a href="#gen:b-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="gen:b-bounds">[gen:b-bounds]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-31"></a>
#### Spectral multiplicities and approximation

<a id="gen:operator-comparison"></a>
<a id="gen:scalar-shift"></a>
For two diagonal spectra of the same size, using the same orthogonal matrix gives the elementary comparison $$\label{gen:operator-comparison}
 |P_N(\Lambda,U)-P_N(\widetilde\Lambda,U)|
 \le\frac12\|\Lambda-\widetilde\Lambda\|_{\mathrm{op}}.$$ It follows by comparing each spin energy and using $|\sigma|^2=N$. The same argument gives monotonicity under coordinatewise increase of the eigenvalues and the exact shift identity $$\label{gen:scalar-shift}
 P_N(\Lambda+c\mathop{\mathrm{Id}},U)=P_N(\Lambda,U)+\frac c2.$$ To change multiplicities, however, a small operator-norm error is not available. The next bound uses rotational symmetry inside each eigenspace.

<a id="stmt-gen-counts"></a>
<div id="gen:counts" class="lemma">

<a id="gen:count-comparison"></a>
**Lemma 29**. *Fix distinct real numbers $\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_m$, and let $L=\max_a\lambda_a-\min_a\lambda_a$. Let $k_a$ and $\widetilde k_a$ be positive integers whose sums are both $N$. For diagonal matrices $\Lambda$ and $\widetilde\Lambda$ with these multiplicities, $$\label{gen:count-comparison}
 \left|\mathbb EP_N(\Lambda,U)-\mathbb EP_N(\widetilde\Lambda,U)\right|
 \le\frac L2\max_a
 \frac{|k_a-\widetilde k_a|}{\min(k_a,\widetilde k_a)}.$$ The same inequality holds for any fixed spin prior on the cube, including a prior with deterministic external fields.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-gen-counts"><summary>Proof of stmt-gen-counts</summary>

*Proof.* Arrange the two diagonal matrices so that, for each $a$, $\min(k_a,\widetilde k_a)$ axes retain the value $\lambda_a$. Haar invariance makes this rearrangement immaterial. Set $\Delta=\widetilde\Lambda-\Lambda$ and $$f(s)=\mathbb EP_N(\Lambda+s\Delta,U),\qquad 0\le s\le1.$$ Convexity of the log partition function gives $f'(0)\le f(1)-f(0)\le f'(1)$. At either endpoint, $$f'(s)=\frac1{2N}\mathbb E\left\langle\sum_i\Delta_{ii}x_i^2
                  \right\rangle_s,
 \qquad x=U\sigma.$$ Within a group of equal endpoint eigenvalues, the expected squared projections are equal on all axes. Indeed, rotating the corresponding rows of $U$ preserves both its Haar law and the spin Hamiltonian; the spin prior is unaffected. If a group has $q_a$ axes and $c_a$ changed axes, its changed axes therefore carry the fraction $c_a/q_a$ of the group’s expected squared spin norm. Since $|\Delta_{ii}|\le L$ and the total squared norm is $N$, $$|f'(s)|\le\frac L2\max_a\frac{c_a}{q_a},
 \qquad s\in\{0,1\}.$$ At the first endpoint $c_a=(k_a-\widetilde k_a)_+$, and at the second $c_a=(\widetilde k_a-k_a)_+$. The endpoint bounds prove <a href="#gen:count-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="gen:count-comparison">[gen:count-comparison]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-gen-approximation"></a>
<div id="gen:approximation" class="proposition">

**Proposition 30**. *Let $\Lambda_N$ be deterministic diagonal matrices. Suppose that their empirical measures converge weakly to a compactly supported probability measure $\mu$, and write $a=\min\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\mu$ and $b=\max\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\mu$. Assume $$\liminf_{N\to\infty}\lambda_{\min}(\Lambda_N)\ge a,
 \qquad
 \limsup_{N\to\infty}\lambda_{\max}(\Lambda_N)\le b.$$ Then $\mathbb EP_N\to\mathcal F(\mu)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-gen-approximation"><summary>Proof of stmt-gen-approximation</summary>
