*Proof.* We first finish the finite-alphabet case. Suppose that $\mu=\sum_{a=1}^m\rho_a\delta_{\lambda_a}$ with all $\rho_a>0$ rational. Choose $n$ so that $s_a=n\rho_a$ are integers. For each residue $r\in\{0,\ldots,n-1\}$, choose nonnegative integers $c_a^r$ with sum $r$, and at dimension $N=kn+r$ use multiplicities $$k_a(N)=ks_a+c_a^r.$$ They increase by $s_a$ when $N$ increases by $n$, and are positive for all sufficiently large $N$. Proposition <a href="#up:bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="up:bound">20</a> and Proposition <a href="#cav:lower" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cav:lower">27</a> give convergence to $\mathcal F(\mu)$ on each progression. There are finitely many residues, so these canonically chosen multiplicities give convergence on the full sequence of dimensions.

Now allow arbitrary positive proportions $\rho_a$ and arbitrary multiplicities $k_a(N)$ with $k_a(N)/N\to\rho_a$. Approximate $\rho$ by positive rational probability vectors $\tau$, and let $\widetilde k_a(N)/N\to\tau_a$ be the canonical counts just constructed. Lemma <a href="#gen:counts" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="gen:counts">29</a> gives $$\begin{aligned}
 &\limsup_{N\to\infty}
 \left|\mathbb EP_N(\Lambda_N,U)-
             \mathcal F\left(\sum_a\tau_a\delta_{\lambda_a}\right)
       \right|\\
 &\hspace{25mm}\le
 \frac L2\max_a\frac{|\rho_a-\tau_a|}{\min(\rho_a,\tau_a)}.
\end{aligned}$$ The right side tends to zero as $\tau\to\rho$. Lemma <a href="#gen:R-continuity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="gen:R-continuity">28</a> applies to these laws because their support is the same fixed alphabet. This proves the assertion for every finite alphabet with positive limiting proportions.

We next construct finite alphabets for the general measure. If $a=b$, then $R_{\delta_a}=a$ and $\mathcal F(\delta_a)=a/2$ because $S\ge0$ and $S(0)=0$; the assertion follows directly from <a href="#gen:operator-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="gen:operator-comparison">[gen:operator-comparison]</a>. Hence suppose $a<b$, and put $$\eta_N=\max\{(a-\lambda_{\min}(\Lambda_N))_+,
                   (\lambda_{\max}(\Lambda_N)-b)_+\}.$$ Then $\eta_N\to0$, and clipping the eigenvalues to $[a,b]$ changes the pressure by at most $\eta_N/2$. The clipped empirical measures still converge weakly to $\mu$.

Fix $\delta>0$. Partition $[a,b]$ into finitely many intervals of length at most $\delta$, with all interior endpoints of zero $\mu$-mass. Use half-open intervals to assign endpoints uniquely, including $a$ and $b$ in the first and last intervals. For each positive-mass interval $I_j$, choose $r_j\in I_j\cap\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\mu$, and put $$\nu_\delta=\sum_{j:\,\mu(I_j)>0}\mu(I_j)\delta_{r_j}.$$ The smallest and largest representatives satisfy $$a\le r_{\min}\le a+\delta,
 \qquad b-\delta\le r_{\max}\le b,$$ because every neighborhood of a support edge has positive mass.

<a id="gen:bin-squeeze"></a>
In a positive-mass interval, replace each clipped eigenvalue by its representative. In all zero-mass intervals together, replace the eigenvalues by $r_{\min}$ to form $\Lambda_N^-$, and by $r_{\max}$ to form $\Lambda_N^+$. Each zero-mass interval is a continuity set for the clipped measures relative to $[a,b]$, so the fraction of eigenvalues in their finite union tends to zero. Both modified spectra therefore have limiting law $\nu_\delta$, with only positive limiting groups. Moreover, coordinatewise, $$\Lambda_N^--(\delta+\eta_N)\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}
 \le\Lambda_N\le
 \Lambda_N^++(\delta+\eta_N)\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}.$$ Monotonicity and <a href="#gen:scalar-shift" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="gen:scalar-shift">[gen:scalar-shift]</a>, followed by the finite-alphabet result, yield $$\label{gen:bin-squeeze}
 \mathcal F(\nu_\delta)-\frac\delta2
 \le\liminf_N\mathbb EP_N
 \le\limsup_N\mathbb EP_N
 \le\mathcal F(\nu_\delta)+\frac\delta2.$$ Thus no estimate involving the fraction changed inside a vanishing group is needed.

As $\delta\downarrow0$, the laws $\nu_\delta$ converge weakly to $\mu$ and their upper support edges converge to $b$. Lemma <a href="#gen:R-continuity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="gen:R-continuity">28</a> gives $\mathcal F(\nu_\delta)\to\mathcal F(\mu)$, and <a href="#gen:bin-squeeze" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="gen:bin-squeeze">[gen:bin-squeeze]</a> completes the proof. The thermodynamic limit was taken for each fixed finite alphabet before its refinement. No derivative bound on the finite-alphabet transforms is required uniformly over those refinements. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-gen-approximation-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-gen-approximation</summary>

*Completion of the proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Proposition <a href="#gen:approximation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="gen:approximation">30</a> supplies convergence of the expected pressures. The spectral hypotheses also imply $\|\Lambda_N\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le K$ for all sufficiently large $N$, for some finite $K$. Comparing the quadratic forms for two orthogonal matrices shows that $P_N(\Lambda_N,U)$ is $K$-Lipschitz in the Frobenius distance, independently of the number of distinct eigenvalues. The fourth-moment argument in Lemma <a href="#rot:concentration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rot:concentration">14</a> therefore gives $$\mathbb E|P_N-\mathbb EP_N|^4\le \frac{C_K}{N^2}.$$ For every $\varepsilon>0$, Markov’s inequality makes the probabilities of $|P_N-\mathbb EP_N|>\varepsilon$ summable. Borel–Cantelli, first for positive rational $\varepsilon$, proves $P_N-\mathbb EP_N\to0$ almost surely. This argument needs no independence between the orthogonal matrices at different dimensions. ◻

</details>

The pressure limit now also gives consequences for random spectra and maximum energies. We derive these first, then apply them to Gaussian-pattern interactions.

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
#### Random spectra and zero temperature

<a id="stmt-gen-random"></a>
<div id="gen:random" class="proposition">

**Proposition 31**. *Let $J_N$ be random real symmetric matrices whose conditional law, given their eigenvalues, is the orthogonal orbit measure. Let $\mu$ be a fixed compactly supported probability measure. If the empirical spectral measures converge weakly to $\mu$ in probability and the two edge excesses in Proposition <a href="#gen:approximation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="gen:approximation">30</a> tend to zero in probability, then $P_N\to\mathcal F(\mu)$ in probability. If these spectral hypotheses hold almost surely, the pressure convergence holds almost surely. In either case, if the random variables $P_N$ are uniformly integrable, then $\mathbb EP_N\to\mathcal F(\mu)$. A sufficient condition for this last hypothesis is uniform integrability of $\|J_N\|_{\mathrm{op}}$, in particular a uniform deterministic bound on these norms.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-gen-random"><summary>Proof of stmt-gen-random</summary>

*Proof.* Let $f_N(\Lambda)$ be the Haar expectation of the pressure with deterministic eigenvalues $\Lambda$. The integral against the conditional orbit law is therefore the finite quantity $f_N(\Lambda_N)$. This is used as a conditional orbit integral, without any assumption of global integrability. Equivalently, all conditional calculations below may first be restricted to $\{\|\Lambda_N\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le K\}$, on which $|P_N|\le K/2$. Under the almost-sure spectral hypotheses, Proposition <a href="#gen:approximation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="gen:approximation">30</a> applies to each spectral sequence in the event of convergence and yields $f_N(\Lambda_N)\to\mathcal F(\mu)$ almost surely. Under the in-probability hypotheses, every subsequence has a further subsequence on which all the spectral hypotheses hold almost surely. Here weak convergence can be expressed by a metric on probability measures, so only finitely many convergence-in-probability assertions are involved. The same deterministic result on this further subsequence proves $f_N(\Lambda_N)\to\mathcal F(\mu)$ in probability.

<a id="gen:localized-concentration"></a>
For each fixed $K$, conditional concentration gives $$\label{gen:localized-concentration}
 \mathbb P\bigl(|P_N-f_N(\Lambda_N)|>\varepsilon,
                   \|\Lambda_N\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le K\bigr)
 \le\frac{C_K}{\varepsilon^4N^2}.$$ The spectral hypotheses in probability imply that the norm bound holds with probability tending to one for a sufficiently large fixed $K$. This proves convergence of the fluctuations in probability. Under the almost-sure hypotheses the spectra are eventually bounded almost surely. Apply Borel–Cantelli to <a href="#gen:localized-concentration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="gen:localized-concentration">[gen:localized-concentration]</a> for integer $K$ and positive rational $\varepsilon$ to obtain almost-sure convergence of the fluctuations as well.

Uniform integrability and convergence in probability imply convergence of expectations. Finally, $|P_N|\le\|J_N\|_{\mathrm{op}}/2$, which proves the sufficient condition. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-gen-ground-state"></a>
<div id="gen:ground-state" class="corollary">

<a id="gen:ground-state-formula"></a>
**Corollary 32**. *Let $J_N=U_N^{\mathsf T}\Lambda_NU_N$ satisfy the deterministic spectral hypotheses of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, with limiting law $\mu$. Define its maximum energy per spin by $$M_N=\frac1{2N}\max_{\sigma\in\{-1,1\}^N}
                         \sigma^{\mathsf T}J_N\sigma.$$ For $\beta>0$, let $\mu_\beta$ be the image of $\mu$ under $\lambda\mapsto\beta\lambda$. Then, almost surely and in expectation, $$\label{gen:ground-state-formula}
 M_N\longrightarrow
 \lim_{\beta\to\infty}\frac{\mathcal F(\mu_\beta)}\beta.$$ In particular, the limit on the right exists.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-gen-ground-state"><summary>Proof of stmt-gen-ground-state</summary>

<a id="gen:maximum-squeeze"></a>
*Proof.* Write $P_N(\beta)$ for the pressure with spectrum $\beta\Lambda_N$. The uniform probability on the cube gives, for every $N$ and $\beta>0$, $$\label{gen:maximum-squeeze}
 \frac{P_N(\beta)}\beta
 \le M_N\le
 \frac{P_N(\beta)}\beta+\frac{\log2}\beta.$$ Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> applies at each positive integer $\beta$. Intersecting these countably many probability-one events, the difference between the limit superior and limit inferior of $M_N$ is at most $\log2/\beta$ for every such integer. Thus $M_N$ converges almost surely. Moreover, its limit belongs to each deterministic interval $$\left[\frac{\mathcal F(\mu_\beta)}\beta,
       \frac{\mathcal F(\mu_\beta)}\beta+\frac{\log2}\beta\right],
 \qquad \beta\in\mathbb N,$$ so it is a deterministic number $M$. The eventual deterministic bound on $\|J_N\|_{\mathrm{op}}$ also gives convergence in expectation. For any real $\beta>0$, take expectations and then $N\to\infty$ in <a href="#gen:maximum-squeeze" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="gen:maximum-squeeze">[gen:maximum-squeeze]</a>. It follows that $$0\le M-\frac{\mathcal F(\mu_\beta)}\beta
 \le\frac{\log2}\beta,$$ which proves <a href="#gen:ground-state-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="gen:ground-state-formula">[gen:ground-state-formula]</a> without a uniform positive-temperature estimate. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-33"></a>
#### Gaussian-pattern Wishart interactions

<a id="gen:wishart-pressure"></a>
Fix $\alpha>0$, put $m_N=\lfloor\alpha N\rfloor$, and let $G_N$ be an $m_N$-by-$N$ matrix of independent standard real Gaussian variables. For $c\in\mathbb R$, define $$\label{gen:wishart-pressure}
 p_N(c)=\frac1N\log\left[
 2^{-N}\sum_{\sigma\in\{-1,1\}^N}
 \exp\left\{\frac{c}{2N}\|G_N\sigma\|_2^2\right\}\right].$$ This is the Gaussian-pattern Hopfield pressure for $J_N=cG_N^{\mathsf T}G_N/N$, with uniform spin probability and the full quadratic form, including its diagonal self-interactions.

Let $a_\alpha=(1-\sqrt\alpha)^2$, $b_\alpha=(1+\sqrt\alpha)^2$, and set <a id="gen:wishart-law"></a>$$\label{gen:wishart-law}
 \begin{gathered}
 \mu_\alpha=(1-\alpha)_+\delta_0+
 \frac{\sqrt{(b_\alpha-x)(x-a_\alpha)}}{2\pi x}
 \mathbf 1_{[a_\alpha,b_\alpha]}(x)\,dx,\\
 \mu_{\alpha,c}=(x\mapsto cx)_\#\mu_\alpha.
 \end{gathered}$$ The displayed density is understood almost everywhere.

<a id="stmt-gen-wishart"></a>
<div id="gen:wishart" class="corollary">

<a id="gen:wishart-limit"></a>
<a id="gen:wishart-zero-temperature"></a>
<a id="gen:gaussian-max-norm"></a>
<a id="gen:gaussian-min-sum"></a>
**Corollary 33** (Gaussian patterns). *For every fixed $c\in\mathbb R$, $$\label{gen:wishart-limit}
 p_N(c)\longrightarrow\mathcal F(\mu_{\alpha,c})$$ in probability and in $L^1$, and hence in expectation. The subsequent zero-temperature limits $$\label{gen:wishart-zero-temperature}
 e_\pm(\alpha):=
 \lim_{\beta\to\infty}\frac{\mathcal F(\mu_{\alpha,\pm\beta})}{\beta}$$ exist, with $e_+(\alpha)\ge0$ and $e_-(\alpha)\le0$. If $g_{N,1},\ldots,g_{N,N}\in\mathbb R^{m_N}$ are the columns of $G_N$, then $$\begin{aligned}
 \frac{\|G_N\|_{\infty\to2}}N
 &=\frac{\|G_N^{\mathsf T}\|_{2\to1}}N
 \longrightarrow\sqrt{2e_+(\alpha)}, \label{gen:gaussian-max-norm}\\
 \frac1N\min_{\sigma\in\{-1,1\}^N}
 \left\|\sum_{i=1}^N\sigma_i g_{N,i}\right\|_2
 &\longrightarrow\sqrt{-2e_-(\alpha)}. \label{gen:gaussian-min-sum}
\end{aligned}$$ Both limits hold in probability and in $L^1$. The operator norms are from $\ell_\infty^N$ to $\ell_2^{m_N}$ and from $\ell_2^{m_N}$ to $\ell_1^N$, respectively.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-gen-wishart"><summary>Proof of stmt-gen-wishart</summary>

*Proof.* Put $W_N=G_N^{\mathsf T}G_N/N$ and $\alpha_N=m_N/N$. For every $Q\in O(N)$, the matrices $G_NQ$ and $G_N$ have the same law. Haar averaging this identity against any bounded function of the ordered eigenvalues shows that the conditional law of $cW_N$ is its orthogonal orbit law, including when the zero eigenvalue is repeated.

The Marchenko–Pastur theorem <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[MarchenkoPastur1967](#ref-MarchenkoPastur1967), Theorem 1 and the examples on pp. 459 and 461] gives weak convergence in probability of the empirical law of $W_N$ to $\mu_\alpha$. To check the edges, let $s_{\min}^+(G_N)$ be the smallest positive singular value. For $m_N\ge1$ and $t>0$, the Gaussian singular-value bounds <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[DavidsonSzarek2001](#ref-DavidsonSzarek2001), Theorem II.13], after scaling and transposing when necessary, give $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb P\left(\frac{s_{\max}(G_N)}{\sqrt N}>
        1+\sqrt{\alpha_N}+t\right)&\le e^{-Nt^2/2},\\
 \mathbb P\left(\frac{s_{\min}^+(G_N)}{\sqrt N}<
        |1-\sqrt{\alpha_N}|-t\right)&\le e^{-Nt^2/2}.
\end{aligned}$$ Together with positive limiting mass near each continuous spectral edge, these bounds give $\lambda_{\max}(W_N)\to b_\alpha$ in probability. The lower edge is $0$ for $\alpha\le1$ and $a_\alpha$ for $\alpha>1$. For $\alpha<1$, $W_N$ has $N-m_N$ zero eigenvalues almost surely for all large $N$; at $\alpha=1$, nonnegativity and limiting mass near zero give the lower-edge limit; for $\alpha>1$, the second bound and limiting mass near $a_\alpha$ give that limit. Thus both edge excesses vanish. Scaling by a fixed $c$ preserves this conclusion, reversing the edges when $c<0$; the case $c=0$ is immediate.

Since $\alpha_N\le\alpha$, integrating the first singular-value bound gives $$\sup_N\mathbb E\|W_N\|_{\mathrm{op}}^2
 =\sup_N\mathbb E\left(\frac{s_{\max}(G_N)}{\sqrt N}\right)^4<\infty.$$ The finitely many possible cases $m_N=0$ are zero matrices. Hence $\|cW_N\|_{\mathrm{op}}$ and $p_N(c)$ are uniformly integrable for each fixed $c$. Proposition <a href="#gen:random" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="gen:random">31</a> gives convergence in probability, and uniform integrability upgrades it to $L^1$.

For $\varepsilon\in\{+1,-1\}$, put $$H_N^\varepsilon=\frac1{2N}
 \max_{\sigma\in\{-1,1\}^N}\sigma^{\mathsf T}(\varepsilon W_N)\sigma,
 \qquad
 A_\beta^\varepsilon=
 \frac{\mathcal F(\mu_{\alpha,\varepsilon\beta})}{\beta}.$$ The same maximum squeeze as in <a href="#gen:maximum-squeeze" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="gen:maximum-squeeze">[gen:maximum-squeeze]</a> holds pointwise: $$\frac{p_N(\varepsilon\beta)}{\beta}
 \le H_N^\varepsilon\le
 \frac{p_N(\varepsilon\beta)}{\beta}+\frac{\log2}{\beta}.$$ Also $|H_N^\varepsilon|\le\|W_N\|_{\mathrm{op}}/2$, so these energies are uniformly integrable. Taking expectations and then $N\to\infty$ at each fixed $\beta>0$ gives $$A_\beta^\varepsilon
 \le\liminf_N\mathbb EH_N^\varepsilon
 \le\limsup_N\mathbb EH_N^\varepsilon
 \le A_\beta^\varepsilon+\frac{\log2}{\beta}.$$ The expectations are bounded, and the gap between their limit superior and limit inferior is at most $\log2/\beta$ for every $\beta>0$. They therefore have a common limit $e_\varepsilon(\alpha)$, and the same bounds give $$0\le e_\varepsilon(\alpha)-A_\beta^\varepsilon
 \le\frac{\log2}{\beta},$$ which proves <a href="#gen:wishart-zero-temperature" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="gen:wishart-zero-temperature">[gen:wishart-zero-temperature]</a>. Given any error tolerance, choose one sufficiently large fixed $\beta$ and use <a href="#gen:wishart-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="gen:wishart-limit">[gen:wishart-limit]</a> in the pointwise squeeze. This proves $H_N^\varepsilon\to e_\varepsilon(\alpha)$ in probability; uniform integrability gives convergence in $L^1$.

Dualizing the Euclidean norm and maximizing a linear functional over the cube gives $$\|G_N\|_{\infty\to2}
 =\|G_N^{\mathsf T}\|_{2\to1}
 =\max_{\sigma\in\{-1,1\}^N}\|G_N\sigma\|_2.$$ Consequently, $$H_N^+=\frac{\|G_N\|_{\infty\to2}^2}{2N^2},
 \qquad
 H_N^-=-\frac1{2N^2}\min_{\sigma\in\{-1,1\}^N}\|G_N\sigma\|_2^2.$$ The signs of $e_+$ and $e_-$ follow from these identities. Since $G_N\sigma=\sum_i\sigma_i g_{N,i}$, the $L^1$ convergence of the energies and the inequality $|\sqrt{x}-\sqrt{y}|\le\sqrt{|x-y|}$ for $x,y\ge0$ give <a href="#gen:gaussian-max-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="gen:gaussian-max-norm">[gen:gaussian-max-norm]</a>–<a href="#gen:gaussian-min-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="gen:gaussian-min-sum">[gen:gaussian-min-sum]</a>. ◻

</details>

For comparison, the finite partition expression in the companion Ising-perceptron manuscript with $f_c(z)=cz^2/2$ is exactly <a href="#gen:wishart-pressure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="gen:wishart-pressure">[gen:wishart-pressure]</a>. For a standard Gaussian $Z$, $\mathbb E|f_c(Z)|=|c|/2$, while $\mathbb Ee^{f_c(Z)}=(1-c)^{-1/2}$ for $c<1$ and is infinite for $c\ge1$. Its integrable-activation theorem <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[OpenAIIsingPerceptron2026](#ref-OpenAIIsingPerceptron2026), Theorem 8.3] therefore applies precisely when $c<1$ in this quadratic family. Uniqueness of the expected-pressure limit gives the equality of variational values $$\mathcal F(\mu_{\alpha,c})=\mathcal P_{f_c}(\alpha),
 \qquad c<1.$$ This compares values, not the functionals pointwise; the companion functional is not invoked for $c\ge1$.

<a id="pm-h-34"></a>
#### Why weak convergence on an unbounded support is insufficient

The bounded-edge hypothesis cannot be replaced by the assertion that all eigenvalues lie in the support of a possibly unbounded limiting law. We give an explicit sequence for which that weaker condition holds but the pressure has no limit.

Let $\mu$ have density $e^\lambda\mathbf 1_{\{\lambda\le0\}}$. For every $N$, its midpoint quantiles are $$q_{N,j}=\log\frac{j-1/2}{N},\qquad 1\le j\le N.$$ Their empirical measures converge weakly to $\mu$. At even $N$ use these eigenvalues. At odd $N$ replace $q_{N,1}$ by $-e^{N^2}$ and leave the other eigenvalues unchanged. Changing one eigenvalue does not affect weak convergence, and every eigenvalue still belongs to $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\mu=(-\infty,0]$.

<a id="gen:even-counterexample"></a>
At even sizes, convexity of $-\log u$ gives the midpoint bound $$\frac1N\sum_{j=1}^N(-q_{N,j})
 \le\int_0^1-\log u\,du=1.$$ Jensen’s inequality for the uniform spin measure, whose mean quadratic energy is $\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\Lambda_N/2$, therefore yields $$\label{gen:even-counterexample}
 -\frac12\le\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\Lambda_N}{2N}\le P_N\le0.$$

<a id="gen:odd-counterexample"></a>
At odd sizes, let $v_N$ be the unit eigenvector corresponding to $-e^{N^2}$. For each fixed spin vector $\sigma$, Haar invariance implies that $v_N\cdot\sigma$ has the distribution of $\sqrt N$ times the first coordinate of a uniform point on the unit sphere in $\mathbb R^N$. For $N\ge3$ its density on a fixed neighborhood of zero is bounded by an absolute constant. Indeed, that density is $$\frac{\Gamma(N/2)}{\sqrt{\pi N}\,\Gamma((N-1)/2)}
 \left(1-\frac{t^2}{N}\right)^{(N-3)/2},
 \qquad |t|<\sqrt N,$$ whose prefactor is bounded. A union bound over the cube gives $$\mathbb P\left(\min_{\sigma\in\{-1,1\}^N}
                  |v_N\cdot\sigma|<e^{-2N}\right)
 \le C2^Ne^{-2N}.$$ This is summable. Almost surely, for all sufficiently large odd $N$, every spin vector therefore has energy at most $-\tfrac12e^{N^2-4N}$, since all remaining eigenvalues are nonpositive. Consequently, $$\label{gen:odd-counterexample}
 P_N\le-\frac{e^{N^2-4N}}{2N}\longrightarrow-\infty
 \qquad\text{along odd }N.$$ The bounds <a href="#gen:even-counterexample" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="gen:even-counterexample">[gen:even-counterexample]</a> and <a href="#gen:odd-counterexample" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="gen:odd-counterexample">[gen:odd-counterexample]</a> prove the claimed failure of convergence. No independence between dimensions is used here either.

<a id="sec:magnetic"></a>
### Extension to an external magnetic field

We now add a deterministic external field. This is a separate extension of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. Its proof uses magnetization constraints: the single-site functional is concave in covariance levels after prescribing the mean spin, and a product of large constrained blocks supplies the corresponding cavity lower bound.

<a id="mag:constrained-functional"></a>
For a deterministic vector $c^{(N)}=(c_1^{(N)},\ldots,c_N^{(N)})\in\mathbb R^N$, write <a id="mag:pressure"></a>$$\label{mag:pressure}
 P_N(c^{(N)})=\frac1N\log\left[
  2^{-N}\sum_{\sigma\in\{-1,1\}^N}
  \exp\left\{\frac12\sigma^{\mathsf T}U^{\mathsf T}\Lambda_NU\sigma
                    +\sum_{r=1}^N c_r^{(N)}\sigma_r\right\}\right].$$ For $b\in\mathbb R$, let $\ell_b^{\mathrm{can}}(h)$ denote the single-site functional defined earlier, with initial position $b$ in place of zero; the subtraction $h_{k-1}/2$ is unchanged. For $s\in(-1,1)$ define $$\label{mag:constrained-functional}
 \ell^{[s]}(h)=\inf_{b\in\mathbb R}
       \{\ell_b^{\mathrm{can}}(h)-bs\}.$$ The bias $b$ is optimized to prescribe the mean spin $s$; the physical external field will contribute its linear term separately in the pressure formula. This single-site concave conjugate is used in generalized TAP theory <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[ChenPanchenkoSubag2023](#ref-ChenPanchenkoSubag2023), 2.3, equations (2.21)–(2.23)]. Optimization over a conventional order parameter outside the Parisi order parameter also appears in Gaussian vector-spin formulas <a id="pm-cite-38"></a>[[ChenConventional2024](#ref-ChenConventional2024), Theorem 1.1]. The covariance-level concavity and the constrained-block comparison needed for the present spectral model are proved in this section.

<a id="mag:Sm"></a>
Given positive rational numbers $\gamma_1,\ldots,\gamma_J$ with sum one and $\mathbf m=(m_1,\ldots,m_J)\in((-1,1)\cap\mathbb Q)^J$, put $$\label{mag:Sm}
 \ell^{[\mathbf m]}(h)=\sum_{j=1}^J\gamma_j\ell^{[m_j]}(h),
 \qquad
 S_{\mathbf m}(p)=\sup_{h\in\mathcal H}
       \left\{\ell^{[\mathbf m]}(h)+\frac12\int_0^1p(s)h(s)\,ds\right\}.$$ The same covariance path $h$ is used in every field group.

For probability laws $\nu,\widetilde\nu$ on $\mathbb R$ with finite absolute first moments, write $$W_1(\nu,\widetilde\nu)
   =\inf_{\pi}\int_{\mathbb R^2}|x-y|\,\pi(dx,dy),$$ where the infimum is over probability laws $\pi$ with marginals $\nu$ and $\widetilde\nu$.

<a id="stmt-mag-theorem"></a>
<div id="mag:theorem" class="theorem">

<a id="mag:finite-formula"></a>
<a id="mag:W1-formula"></a>
**Theorem 34** (Magnetic-field extension). *Assume the deterministic spectral hypotheses of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, with limiting compactly supported law $\mu$. Suppose first that the field values belong to a fixed finite alphabet $c_1,\ldots,c_J\in\mathbb R$, and that the proportion of sites with value $c_j$ tends to a positive rational number $\gamma_j$. Then $P_N(c^{(N)})$ converges in expectation and almost surely to $$\label{mag:finite-formula}
 \mathcal F_\mu\left(\sum_{j=1}^J\gamma_j\delta_{c_j}\right)
 =\sup_{\mathbf m\in((-1,1)\cap\mathbb Q)^J}
   \left\{\sum_{j=1}^J\gamma_jc_jm_j
    +\inf_{p\in\mathcal P}\bigl[S_{\mathbf m}(p)+G_1(p)\bigr]\right\}.$$ More generally, suppose the empirical field laws converge in the first-moment transport metric $W_1$ to a probability law $\nu$ on $\mathbb R$ with finite absolute first moment. Then the same modes of convergence hold, with limit $\mathcal F_\mu(\nu)$, where $$\label{mag:W1-formula}
 \mathcal F_\mu(\nu)
   =\lim_{q\to\infty}\mathcal F_\mu(\nu_q)$$ for any finite-support laws $\nu_q$ with positive rational masses such that $W_1(\nu_q,\nu)\to0$. This limit exists and is independent of the approximating sequence.*

</div>

<a id="stmt-mag-random-data"></a>
<div id="mag:random-data" class="remark">

*Remark 35* (Conditional random spectral and field data). Let the spectrum and the field vector both be random. Conditional jointly on these data, assume that the interaction has the Haar orbit law with the prescribed spectrum. Let $\mu$ be a fixed compactly supported spectral law and $\nu$ a fixed field law with finite absolute first moment. If the spectral hypotheses of Equation <a href="#eq:spectral-assumptions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:spectral-assumptions">[eq:spectral-assumptions]</a> and convergence of the empirical field laws to $\nu$ in $W_1$ hold in probability, then $P_N(c^{(N)})\to\mathcal F_\mu(\nu)$ in probability. If both data convergences hold almost surely, the pressure convergence also holds almost surely. In either case, uniform integrability of the pressures implies convergence of their expectations.

Indeed, condition on both data sets and apply the deterministic theorem along convergent data sequences. The subsequence argument and localized fourth-moment concentration in Proposition <a href="#gen:random" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="gen:random">31</a> apply unchanged, since the Haar Lipschitz constant is independent of the external field. The conditional Haar hypothesis remains essential when the fields and the interaction are dependent.

</div>

<a id="pm-h-36"></a>
#### Concavity at prescribed magnetization

We first prove the concavity needed to optimize the cavity expression. All level derivatives in this subsection keep the interval weights $w_i$ fixed. For a fixed bias $b$, let $Z_i$ be the tilted Gaussian position chain, starting before the root increment at $b$, and let $F_i$ be its unshifted backward functions. Define $$\mathcal B_i(h,b)=\mathbb E\bigl[F_i'(Z_i)^2\bigr].$$ The terminal conditional mean spin is $\tanh Z_{k-1}$, and its successive conditional means are $F_i'(Z_i)$. Consequently these squared-mean expectations are nonnegative and nondecreasing in $i$.

<a id="stmt-mag-concavity"></a>
<div id="mag:concavity" class="proposition">

<a id="mag:envelope-derivative"></a>
**Proposition 36**. *Fix $s\in(-1,1)$ and positive interval weights $w_0,\ldots,w_{k-1}$. The function $\ell^{[s]}$ is concave on $0\le h_0\le\cdots\le h_{k-1}<\infty$. Its minimizing bias $b_s(h)$ in <a href="#mag:constrained-functional" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mag:constrained-functional">[mag:constrained-functional]</a> is unique. On strict level data, set $$B_i(h,s)=\mathcal B_i(h,b_s(h)).$$ Then $$\label{mag:envelope-derivative}
 \partial_{h_i}\ell^{[s]}(h)=-\frac{w_i}{2}B_i(h,s).$$ The associated supporting inequalities extend to boundary level data and are unchanged by subdivision of intervals with tied covariance levels. The function is $1/2$-Lipschitz in the $L^1$ distance between bounded nonnegative nondecreasing paths.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-mag-concavity"><summary>Proof of stmt-mag-concavity</summary>

<a id="mag:bias-bounds"></a>
*Proof.* Let $f_h(b)=\ell_b^{\mathrm{can}}(h)+h_{k-1}/2$ be the unshifted initial backward value. Gaussian convolution and differentiation of the log-exponential operators show that $f_h$ is smooth and strictly convex: its second derivative is a positive tilted average of the terminal second derivative, plus nonnegative conditional-variance terms. Moreover, with $H=h_{k-1}$, $$\label{mag:bias-bounds}
 \log\cosh b\le f_h(b)\le\log\cosh b+H/2.$$ For the lower bound, compare every backward operator with ordinary expectation and use convexity of $\log\cosh$. For the upper bound, compare with exponent one throughout and use $\mathbb E\cosh(b+\sqrt H Z)=e^{H/2}\cosh b$. Convex difference quotients in <a href="#mag:bias-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mag:bias-bounds">[mag:bias-bounds]</a> imply that $f_h'(b)\to\pm1$ as $b\to\pm\infty$, uniformly for $H$ in a bounded set. Thus $f_h'(b_s(h))=s$ determines a unique bias, bounded uniformly over paths of bounded height for each fixed interior $s$.

Gaussian differentiation, as in Proposition <a href="#fld:derivative" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fld:derivative">9</a>, gives $\partial_{h_i}\ell_b^{\mathrm{can}}=-w_i\mathcal B_i(h,b)/2$. The envelope rule proves <a href="#mag:envelope-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mag:envelope-derivative">[mag:envelope-derivative]</a>. In the remainder of the proof $s$ is held fixed, so derivatives of $B_i(h,s)$ include the change in $b_s(h)$. We use the mean spin as spatial coordinate, so that the varying bias is represented by the fixed initial coordinate $s$. We will prove that $\partial_{h_l}B_i\le0$ for $l>i$ and $\sum_l h_l\partial_{h_l}B_i\ge0$, then apply the Hessian argument of Theorem <a href="#fld:concavity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fld:concavity">12</a>.

<a id="mag:inverse-PDE"></a>
<a id="mag:continuation-PDE"></a>
Set $T=h_{k-1}$ and let $\zeta(v)=\zeta_i$ for $h_{i-1}<v<h_i$. The continuous-time version of the unshifted backward recursion solves, on each such interval, $$F_v+\frac12F_{zz}+\frac12\zeta(v)F_z^2=0,
 \qquad F(T,z)=\log\cosh z.$$ For the radial comparison we also need $\alpha>0$. Let $F^\alpha$ solve the same recursion with terminal value $\alpha^{-1}\log\cosh(\alpha z)$ and coefficient $\alpha\zeta(v)$ in place of $\zeta(v)$. Write $$u=F_z^\alpha(v,z),\qquad
 a(v,u)=F_{zz}^\alpha(v,z(v,u)),\qquad -1<u<1.$$ Strict convexity gives the inverse $z(v,u)$. With $L=\partial_v+\tfrac12\partial_{zz}
             +\alpha\zeta F_z^\alpha\partial_z$, differentiation gives $L(F_z^\alpha)=0$ and $L(F_{zz}^\alpha)=-\alpha\zeta(F_{zz}^\alpha)^2$. The chain rule in inverse coordinates therefore yields $$\label{mag:inverse-PDE}
 a_v+\frac12a^2a_{uu}=-\alpha\zeta(v)a^2,
 \qquad a(T,u)=\alpha(1-u^2).$$ Likewise, continuation of a payoff by the tilted transition kernels satisfies $$\label{mag:continuation-PDE}
 H_v+\frac12a^2H_{uu}=0.$$ In particular, if $H_i$ has terminal condition $H_i(h_i,u)=u^2$, then $B_i(h,s)=H_i(0,s)$ at $\alpha=1$.

We record the boundary regularity that permits comparison on the closed interval $[-1,1]$. For fixed strict level data and $\alpha>0$, the backward functions have, as $z\to+\infty$, expansions to every prescribed finite order of the form <a id="mag:asymptotic"></a>$$\label{mag:asymptotic}
 F^\alpha(v,z)
  =z+C(v)+c_1(v)e^{-2\alpha z}+\cdots
       +c_M(v)e^{-2M\alpha z}
       +O(e^{-2(M+1)\alpha z}),\qquad c_1(v)>0.$$ The expansions hold with any fixed finite number of differentiated remainders, uniformly on the closed time intervals between successive levels. To verify this, start from the expansion of the terminal $\alpha^{-1}\log\cosh(\alpha z)$. An ordinary Gaussian backward step integrates each exponential term. In a positive-exponent step, first factor the linear exponential, expand the remaining exponential, integrate term by term against the Gaussian with the resulting shifted mean, and take the logarithm. The leading correction is multiplied by a strictly positive Gaussian exponential moment. Remainders satisfy the same bounds: on bounded or negative arguments an exponential bound of the indicated order dominates their linear or exponential growth, and Gaussian exponential moments are finite. The argument applies after differentiating any fixed finite number of times. Coefficients are continuous in time, and the positive leading coefficient is bounded away from zero for the fixed data under consideration.

Tilted continuations of $(F_z^\alpha)^2$ obey the same finite expansion property, by taking ratios of the corresponding Gaussian integrals. Since $1-u=2\alpha c_1(v)e^{-2\alpha z}+O(e^{-4\alpha z})$, inverting this expansion gives bounded spatial derivatives through order four for $a$ and the functions $H_i$ on $[-1,1]$. Reflection treats the other endpoint. In particular, $$a(v,u)=2\alpha(1-u)+O((1-u)^2)\quad(u\to1),
 \qquad
 a(v,u)=2\alpha(1+u)+O((1+u)^2)\quad(u\to-1).$$ Thus $a$ vanishes at both endpoints, as does $(a^2)_u$. The equations give the required one-sided time derivatives at the finitely many level boundaries.

We next prove two monotonicities. First, every $H_i$ is convex in $u$. Indeed $W=H_{i,uu}$ satisfies $$W_v+\frac12(a^2W)_{uu}=0,
 \qquad W(h_i,u)=2.$$ Expanded as a linear equation, its coefficients are bounded; the diffusion and first-derivative coefficients vanish at the endpoints. Backward comparison preserves nonnegativity. The same comparison in <a href="#mag:continuation-PDE" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mag:continuation-PDE">[mag:continuation-PDE]</a> shows that increasing a nonnegative coefficient $a$ pointwise increases $H_i$, because its second spatial derivative is nonnegative. These comparison assertions also follow directly by reversing time, multiplying a proposed difference by a sufficiently decaying exponential, and excluding a first negative minimum; the bounded zeroth-order terms are absorbed by the exponential. This argument applies interval by interval in time, including the degenerate endpoints.

<a id="mag:off-diagonal"></a>
At $\alpha=1$, fix $l>i$. The covariance derivative calculation for the backward field value, at any $v\le h_i$, reads $$\partial_{h_l}F(v,z)
   =\text{a constant independent of }z
       -\frac{w_l}{2}H_l(v,F_z(v,z)).$$ For a variation $\delta F$, differentiation at fixed inverse coordinate gives <a id="mag:inverse-variation"></a>$$\label{mag:inverse-variation}
 \delta a
   =\delta F_{zz}-\frac{F_{zzz}}{F_{zz}}\delta F_z
   =a^2\partial_{uu}\bigl[\delta F(v,z(v,u))\bigr].$$ Convexity of $H_l$ therefore implies $\partial_{h_l}a\le0$ through time $h_i$. Applying the preceding continuation comparison at the fixed initial coordinate $s$ proves $$\label{mag:off-diagonal}
 \partial_{h_l}B_i(h,s)\le0\qquad(l>i).$$

<a id="mag:radial"></a>
For the second monotonicity, $a$ increases with $\alpha$. To see the sign explicitly, reverse time in <a href="#mag:inverse-PDE" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mag:inverse-PDE">[mag:inverse-PDE]</a>, write $A_1,A_2$ for the solutions with $\alpha_1<\alpha_2$, and set $D=A_2-A_1$. Then $$D_t=\frac12A_2^2D_{uu}+cD
             +(\alpha_2-\alpha_1)\zeta A_1^2,
 \qquad
 c=(A_2+A_1)\left(\frac12A_{1,uu}+\alpha_2\zeta\right).$$ The coefficient $c$ is bounded, the initial difference is $(\alpha_2-\alpha_1)(1-u^2)\ge0$, and the boundary difference is zero. Comparison proves $D\ge0$. Position and time rescaling give the identity $$F_h^\alpha(v,z)
    =\alpha^{-1}F_{\alpha^2h}^{1}(\alpha^2v,\alpha z),$$ with the level intervals rescaled on the right. The corresponding initial biases satisfy $b_s^\alpha(h)=\alpha^{-1}b_s^1(\alpha^2h)$. It follows that increasing $\alpha$ increases $B_i(\alpha^2h,s)$, whence $$\label{mag:radial}
 \sum_l h_l\partial_{h_l}B_i(h,s)\ge0.$$

Finally, the matrix $M_{il}=w_i\partial_{h_l}B_i$ is symmetric by <a href="#mag:envelope-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mag:envelope-derivative">[mag:envelope-derivative]</a>. Equations <a href="#mag:off-diagonal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mag:off-diagonal">[mag:off-diagonal]</a> and <a href="#mag:radial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mag:radial">[mag:radial]</a> show that $\operatorname{diag}(h)M\operatorname{diag}(h)$ has nonpositive off-diagonal entries and nonnegative row sums. For a symmetric matrix with these properties its quadratic form is a sum of nonnegative weighted squares of coordinate differences and nonnegative diagonal terms. Thus $M$ is positive semidefinite on strict level data, proving concavity. Continuity extends the supporting inequalities to the boundary. Tied levels introduce no new conditional mean and simply repeat the same $B_i$. The bound $0\le B_i\le1$ in <a href="#mag:envelope-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mag:envelope-derivative">[mag:envelope-derivative]</a>, followed by common refinement, gives the $L^1$ Lipschitz assertion. ◻

</details>

We shall use the weighted mean $$B_i^*(h)=\sum_{j=1}^J\gamma_jB_i(h,m_j).$$ Proposition <a href="#mag:concavity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="mag:concavity">36</a> gives the supporting derivative $-w_iB_i^*(h)/2$ for $\ell^{[\mathbf m]}$.

<a id="pm-h-37"></a>
#### Upper bounds on magnetization slices

We first work with a finite spectral alphabet of positive rational proportions, along the count progressions used in the cavity construction. We also arrange periodic field proportions $\gamma_j$, with bounded remainder sites. The final subsection removes these restrictions. Fix an interior rational vector $\mathbf m$. Let $\mathcal I_{j,N}$ be the sites in field group $j$ and put $N_j=|\mathcal I_{j,N}|$. For all sufficiently large $N$, choose integers $0\le k_{j,N}\le N_j$ such that $2k_{j,N}/N_j-1\to m_j$; for example, take the nearest integer to $N_j(1+m_j)/2$. Define the exact slice $$\mathcal S_N(\mathbf m)=
 \left\{\sigma\in\{-1,1\}^N:
   \#\{r\in\mathcal I_{j,N}:\sigma_r=1\}=k_{j,N}
   \text{ for every }j\right\}.$$ Retain its original cube mass $2^{-N}\mathbf 1_{\mathcal S_N(\mathbf m)}$.

The concentration, perturbation, and rotation identities of Section <a href="#sec:rotation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:rotation">4</a> apply to this prior: after normalizing its nonzero mass, it is a deterministic probability on cube spins independent of the Haar frame. The logarithm of its mass is kept in the pressure. At interpolation time zero, insert an arbitrary bias $b_j$ on field group $j$. This adds a constant on the slice. Relaxing the slice constraint then bounds its expected enriched pressure by $$\sum_j\gamma_j\ell_{b_j}^{\mathrm{can}}(h)
                -\sum_j\gamma_jb_jm_j+o(1).$$ Minimizing in the biases gives $\ell^{[\mathbf m]}(h)+o(1)$, uniformly on fixed-step compact field sets. Uniformity follows from the bounded-bias conclusion of <a href="#mag:bias-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mag:bias-bounds">[mag:bias-bounds]</a> and the vanishing rounding errors in the group proportions and magnetizations.

<a id="mag:slice-upper"></a>
The contact comparison of Section <a href="#sec:upper" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:upper">5</a> now gives $$\label{mag:slice-upper}
 \limsup_{N\to\infty}\mathbb EP_{N,\mathcal S_N(\mathbf m)}(0)
      \le\inf_{p\in\mathcal P}\{S_{\mathbf m}(p)+G_1(p)\}.$$ Here $P_{N,\mathcal S}(0)$ denotes the pressure with the original cube mass restricted to $\mathcal S$ and with no external field. We specify the changes in that comparison. For a step trial $q$ of maximum less than one, choosing bias zero in <a href="#mag:constrained-functional" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mag:constrained-functional">[mag:constrained-functional]</a> and testing $h=0$ in <a href="#mag:Sm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mag:Sm">[mag:Sm]</a> give $$-\log2\le S_{\mathbf m}(q)\le S(q)<\infty,$$ where finiteness follows from Lemma <a href="#fld:domain" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fld:domain">13</a>. Replace $S(q)$ in the minimizing objective by $S_{\mathbf m}(q)$. The time-zero bound just proved excludes that endpoint. Relaxing the slice without any bias gives the same large-height estimate as before, with at most an additional $\log2$ in its constant lower bound, so the field cap is excluded as well. The field derivative at a minimum still gives the tail inequalities between the Gibbs quantile $p$ and $q$; the rotation identities still give the energy comparison, excluding a minimum at positive time. These are precisely the three conclusions used in the contact argument. The small perturbation changes the pressure by $O(e_N)$ also for the normalized slice prior. Rounding an arbitrary $p$ downward cannot increase $S_{\mathbf m}(p)$, and changes $G_1(p)$ continuously, proving <a href="#mag:slice-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mag:slice-upper">[mag:slice-upper]</a> for the full trial class.

It remains to justify the supremum over rational interior slices in the full partition function. If two prescribed sets of positive-spin counts differ by at most $\delta N$, map every configuration of the first slice to the second by flipping at most that many spins. For a uniformly bounded interaction matrix $J_N=U^{\mathsf T}\Lambda_NU$, $$\frac1{2N}\left|\sigma^{\mathsf T}J_N\sigma
                       -\tau^{\mathsf T}J_N\tau\right|
 \le C\sqrt\delta.$$ Indeed $\|\sigma-\tau\|\le2\sqrt{\delta N}$ and $\|\sigma+\tau\|\le2\sqrt N$. Every image has at most the size of a Hamming ball of radius $\delta N$ preimages. Applying maps in both directions shows that the two restricted normalized log partitions differ by at most $C\sqrt\delta+\mathsf h(\delta)+o_N(1)$ for small $\delta$, where $\mathsf h(x)=-x\log x-(1-x)\log(1-x)$. The external-field contributions differ by at most $2\max_j|c_j|\delta$.

Choose a finite arbitrarily fine net of rational interior magnetizations, also approximating the endpoint values $\pm1$. There are at most $(N+1)^J$ slices, so summing their partition functions adds only $J\log(N+1)/N$. The preceding deterministic comparison reduces their maximum to the finite net. Concentration from Lemma <a href="#rot:concentration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rot:concentration">14</a> applies to every member of that net. Equation <a href="#mag:slice-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mag:slice-upper">[mag:slice-upper]</a>, followed by refinement of the net, therefore proves the upper bound in <a href="#mag:finite-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mag:finite-formula">[mag:finite-formula]</a>, in expectation and almost surely.

<a id="pm-h-38"></a>
#### A product of constrained blocks

For the lower bound, fix $\mathbf m$ as above and choose an integer $n$ such that $n\gamma_j$, $n\gamma_j(1+m_j)/2$, and $n\rho_a$ are all integers. Integers $n$ with these properties have unbounded multiples. Partition the regular sites into blocks of size $n$, with the same field group composition in each block. In every block prescribe magnetization $m_j$ separately in group $j$. Freeze any bounded number of remainder sites. This gives a product of nonempty finite slices; retain the original cube mass throughout.

For this fixed $n$, add one block at a time using the coupling of Section <a href="#sec:cavity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:cavity">6</a>. The spectral coupling, fresh within-group frames, perturbation comparison, and quadratic Gaussian transformation are unchanged. Their base-replica moment bounds use only independence of the fresh frames and $\|\sigma\|^2=N$, so they apply to the present prior. For clarity, the two uses of full-model site symmetry have the following replacements.

First, each position within a regular block is exchangeable with the same position in every other regular block. If there are $K$ such blocks, the expected square of a spectral projection at any fixed regular position is at most $(N+n)/K$. For fixed $n$ this is bounded uniformly in $N$, and the finite number of cavity coordinates consequently has the projection tightness required for the restricted Gibbs tests. Second, averaging a cavity spin product over its $n$ positions, against a bounded test of the total overlap, equals the analogous average over all regular sites. The difference from the full total overlap is bounded by twice the fraction of remainder sites. These observations give exactly the tightness and overlap-test identities used in Lemma <a href="#cav:comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cav:comparison">22</a> and Proposition <a href="#cav:self-consistency" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cav:self-consistency">26</a>.

If a restricted spin integral is empty for some frame data, assign zero to its bounded Gibbs test. The full tilted mass outside the restriction bounds the error of this convention. On the base side, the same moment bounds make the probability of a nearly empty restricted mass small, and denominator regularization proceeds as in Lemma <a href="#cav:comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cav:comparison">22</a>. For logarithms, Jensen now includes the logarithm of the positive cavity-slice mass, a constant depending on the fixed $n$. Thus the uniform lower bound for increments and all cap-removal steps remain valid. No estimate uniform in $n$ is required at this point.

Let $\mathcal C_n\subset\{-1,1\}^n$ be the constrained cavity block. Define $\ell^{(n)}(h)$ by the same backward Gaussian recursion as $\ell$, now with $n$ independent coordinate increments and terminal payoff $$\log\left(2^{-n}\sum_{\varepsilon\in\mathcal C_n}
                       e^{z\cdot\varepsilon}\right).$$ Subtract $nh_{k-1}/2$ and divide the resulting initial value by $n$. Let $B_i^{(n)}(h)$ be the mean over the $n$ coordinates of the expected squared conditional spin means at level $i$. It is a nonnegative nondecreasing sequence in $[0,1]$, and Gaussian differentiation gives $d\ell^{(n)}=-\tfrac12\sum_iw_iB_i^{(n)}dh_i$. For each fixed $n$, the cavity-spin tests will match these block conditional products to the limiting overlap quantile. We then let the block size grow. Uniform approximation of both $\ell^{(n)}$ and $B^{(n)}$ by $\ell^{[\mathbf m]}$ and $B^*$ will allow the supporting inequality of Proposition <a href="#mag:concavity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="mag:concavity">36</a> to control the field supremum. Thus concavity is used for $\ell^{[\mathbf m]}$ after the block replacement; concavity of $\ell^{(n)}$ is not required.

<a id="mag:hp"></a>
<a id="mag:block-cavity"></a>
For a limiting base quantile $p$, take the finite monotone roundings $\bar p$ used in Section <a href="#sec:cavity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:cavity">6</a>, and set $$\label{mag:hp}
 h_{\bar p}(s)=\int_0^{\bar p(s)}R'(D_{\bar p}(r))\,dr.$$ The evaluation in Proposition <a href="#cav:evaluation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cav:evaluation">25</a> gives the cavity expression per added spin $$\label{mag:block-cavity}
 \ell^{(n)}(h_{\bar p})
       +\frac12\int_0^1\bar p(s)h_{\bar p}(s)\,ds+G_1(\bar p).$$ The determinant term is unchanged. The residual covariance and the matrix $C$ on the cavity coordinates are scalar, so their contributions depend only on $\|\varepsilon\|^2=n$ and remain constant on the slice.

<a id="mag:block-consistency"></a>
At every parameter sequence for which the averaged perturbation estimates of Section <a href="#sec:rotation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:rotation">4</a> vanish, a subsequential base overlap limit exists. The cap argument gives a lower bound for the liminf increment by the limit of <a href="#mag:block-cavity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mag:block-cavity">[mag:block-cavity]</a>. The bounded Gibbs tests and the cavity-site average proved above give, for every continuous $\phi$, $$\lim_{\bar p\to p}\int_0^1\phi(\bar p(s))
       \bigl(B^{(n)}_{h_{\bar p}}(s)-\bar p(s)\bigr)\,ds=0.$$ As in Proposition <a href="#cav:self-consistency" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cav:self-consistency">26</a>, monotonicity gives compactness of the $B^{(n)}$ paths. A limiting path is constant on each plateau of $p$, since equal covariance levels repeat conditional means. It is therefore a function of $p$, and the test identity forces $$\label{mag:block-consistency}
 B^{(n)}_{h_{\bar p}}\longrightarrow p\quad\hbox{in }L^1.$$ Gaussian comparison also gives the $L^1$ continuity needed for the limit of <a href="#mag:block-cavity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mag:block-cavity">[mag:block-cavity]</a>. These statements take $N\to\infty$ and then remove caps and refine the finite quantiles, with $n$ fixed.

The remaining task is to replace the block functional by the constrained single-site functional. Its proof must be uniform in the number of cascade levels, because the roundings of $p$ can have arbitrarily many steps.

<a id="stmt-mag-replacement"></a>
<div id="mag:replacement" class="lemma">

<a id="mag:replacement-estimates"></a>
**Lemma 37** (Uniform replacement of a constrained block). *Fix an interior rational $\mathbf m$, positive rational proportions $\gamma_j$, and a height bound $H<\infty$. Along integers $n$ for which the block constraints are attainable, $$\label{mag:replacement-estimates}
 \sup_{\substack{h\in\mathcal H\\\|h\|_\infty\le H}}
       |\ell^{(n)}(h)-\ell^{[\mathbf m]}(h)|\longrightarrow0,
 \qquad
 \sup_{\substack{h\in\mathcal H\\\|h\|_\infty\le H}}
       \max_i|B_i^{(n)}(h)-B_i^*(h)|\longrightarrow0.$$ The estimates are uniform in the number of steps and their positive weights.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-mag-replacement"><summary>Proof of stmt-mag-replacement</summary>

<a id="mag:root-leaf-loss"></a>
*Proof.* For a given path $h$, insert the minimizing bias $b_j=b_{m_j}(h)$ in every coordinate of group $j$. These biases are bounded uniformly by <a href="#mag:bias-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mag:bias-bounds">[mag:bias-bounds]</a>. Consider two unnormalized $n$-coordinate recursions: the canonical one with all $2^n$ spins, and the constrained one with $\mathcal C_n$. Both use these same biases. Denote the backward values just after level $i$ by $U_i$ and $V_i$, respectively, and put $\Delta_i=U_i-V_i\ge0$. The canonical values add over coordinates, so its tilted kernels factor over sites. Write $$L_n=U_{-1}(0)-V_{-1}(0)
    =n\bigl(\ell^{[\mathbf m]}(h)-\ell^{(n)}(h)\bigr).$$ We first prove $L_n=o(n)$ uniformly over the permitted paths, which gives the first estimate. The same loss will bound the relative entropy of two sampled paths; independent-site concentration under the canonical law will then give the second estimate. At the root, $L_n=\mathbb E\Delta_0$. At a positive-exponent step the canonical transition kernel, denoted by $K_i^{\mathrm{can}}$, gives $$\Delta_{i-1}
  =-\frac1{\zeta_i}\log
       \mathbb E_{K_i^{\mathrm{can}}}e^{-\zeta_i\Delta_i}
  \le\mathbb E_{K_i^{\mathrm{can}}}\Delta_i.$$ Iteration proves $$\label{mag:root-leaf-loss}
 0\le L_n\le\mathbb E_{\mathrm{can}}\Delta_{k-1}.$$

<a id="mag:increment-mgf"></a>
<a id="mag:field-moment"></a>
We bound the terminal loss without a factor depending on the number of steps. If a one-coordinate canonical increment $g$ has Gaussian variance $v$, its conditional tilted density is proportional to $\exp\{\zeta F(z+g)\}$, where $F$ is $1$-Lipschitz and $0\le\zeta\le1$. Gaussian shifting gives, for every $t\in\mathbb R$, $$\label{mag:increment-mgf}
 \mathbb E_{\mathrm{tilt}}e^{tg}
  =e^{t^2v/2}
    \frac{\mathbb Ee^{\zeta F(z+g+tv)}}{\mathbb Ee^{\zeta F(z+g)}}
  \le e^{t^2v/2+|t|v}.$$ For the root increment the sharper ordinary Gaussian bound holds. Successive conditioning in <a href="#mag:increment-mgf" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mag:increment-mgf">[mag:increment-mgf]</a> bounds the exponential moments of each terminal field using only total variance $H$ and the bounded initial bias. Thus, for the vector $z$ of terminal fields including their biases, $$\label{mag:field-moment}
 \mathbb E_{\mathrm{can}}\|z\|^2\le C_H n,$$ uniformly in the depth and the step weights.

<a id="mag:loss-rate"></a>
Under the averaged canonical path kernels and final spin draws, the sites are independent and their means are $m_j$ in group $j$; this is exactly the bias equation $f_h'(b_j)=m_j$. Map each spin vector $\varepsilon$ deterministically to a nearest vector $\widehat\varepsilon\in\mathcal C_n$. If $D$ is the number of flipped coordinates, the binomial variance estimate in each group gives $\mathbb E_{\mathrm{can}}D\le C\sqrt n$. At fixed fields, use the pushforward of the canonical Gibbs spin law as a trial law for the constrained log partition. The finite-space log-sum variational identity then bounds the terminal loss by the expected exponent loss plus the entropy loss. Averaging the former and using <a href="#mag:field-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mag:field-moment">[mag:field-moment]</a>, $$\mathbb E\bigl|z\cdot(\varepsilon-\widehat\varepsilon)\bigr|
 \le(\mathbb E\|z\|^2)^{1/2}(4\mathbb ED)^{1/2}
 \le C_H n^{3/4}.$$ The original spin vector is determined by its image and its flip mask. Therefore its entropy loss is at most the entropy of that mask, and subadditivity and concavity of binary entropy give the averaged bound $n\mathsf h(\mathbb ED/n)\le C\sqrt n\log(n+1)$. Together with <a href="#mag:root-leaf-loss" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mag:root-leaf-loss">[mag:root-leaf-loss]</a>, this proves $$\label{mag:loss-rate}
 0\le L_n\le C_H\bigl(n^{3/4}+\sqrt n\log(n+1)\bigr)=o(n).$$ This establishes the first assertion of <a href="#mag:replacement-estimates" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mag:replacement-estimates">[mag:replacement-estimates]</a>.

<a id="mag:weighted-entropy"></a>
To compare the two-spin products, compare the path-mark and terminal-spin laws under the constrained and canonical kernels. The logarithm of the ratio of their level-$i$ densities is $\zeta_i(\Delta_{i-1}-\Delta_i)$ for $i>0$. The root mark law is unchanged. On allowed terminal spins, the final log-density ratio is $\Delta_{k-1}$. Hence the chain rule for conditional relative entropies, with weight $1/\zeta_i$ at level $i>0$ and weight one for the final spin draw, telescopes to $L_n$: $$\label{mag:weighted-entropy}
 \sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\frac1{\zeta_i}
       \mathbb E_{\mathrm{con}}\mathop{\mathrm{KL}}(K_i^{\mathrm{con}}\Vert K_i^{\mathrm{can}})
 +\mathbb E_{\mathrm{con}}\mathop{\mathrm{KL}}(K_{\mathrm{spin}}^{\mathrm{con}}
                                \Vert K_{\mathrm{spin}}^{\mathrm{can}})
 =L_n.$$ Here each conditional entropy is evaluated at the same past, with the outer expectation taken under the constrained past law. Every term is nonnegative. Since $\zeta_i\le1$, the ordinary relative entropy of the full one-path laws is at most $L_n$. This bound remains valid for arbitrarily small positive $\zeta_i$; no lower bound on the step weights or their cumulative sums is used.

Condition now on a specified common level $i$ of two sampled cascade leaves. The transformed cascade law and the mark representation of Proposition <a href="#arr:rpc" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="arr:rpc">5</a> show that each path has the same one-path marginal; its kernels are shared before the split and independent afterwards. The conditional relative entropies of the shared kernels are counted once and those after the split, including terminal spin draws, at most twice. Thus the joint two-path relative entropy is at most $2L_n$. In the canonical law, sites are independent even conditional on this branching information: the backward values add over sites and each kernel factors over their coordinates. The average two-spin product therefore has mean $B_i^*(h)$, and the elementary exponential-moment bound for independent variables in $[-1,1]$ yields $$\mathbb P_{\mathrm{can}}\left\{
   \left|\frac1n\sum_{r=1}^n\varepsilon_r^1\varepsilon_r^2-B_i^*(h)\right|
                      >\eta\right\}
     \le2e^{-n\eta^2/2}.$$ The constants do not depend on the branching level or the depth. For any event $A$ and probability laws $\nu,\pi$, $$\mathop{\mathrm{KL}}(\nu\Vert\pi)
       \ge\nu(A)\log\frac1{\pi(A)}-\log2,$$ as follows by mapping to the two events $A,A^c$ and expanding binary relative entropy. Apply this with the constrained and canonical two-path laws. Equations <a href="#mag:loss-rate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mag:loss-rate">[mag:loss-rate]</a> and <a href="#mag:weighted-entropy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mag:weighted-entropy">[mag:weighted-entropy]</a> imply that every fixed positive deviation has constrained probability tending to zero uniformly in $i$ and $h$. The average spin product is bounded by one, so its expectation converges uniformly to $B_i^*(h)$. Its constrained expectation is $B_i^{(n)}(h)$, which proves the second assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-39"></a>
#### Completing the lower bound and the approximations

<a id="mag:block-tangent"></a>
<a id="mag:truncated-infimum"></a>
For the fixed finite spectral alphabet, $R'$ is bounded on $[0,1]$. The fields in <a href="#mag:hp" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mag:hp">[mag:hp]</a> consequently have a common height bound $H$ independent of $p$ and its rounding. Fix a height $A<\infty$ for a test field $v\in\mathcal H$. By Proposition <a href="#mag:concavity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="mag:concavity">36</a> and Lemma <a href="#mag:replacement" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="mag:replacement">37</a>, after a common refinement of step intervals, $$\label{mag:block-tangent}
 \ell^{[\mathbf m]}(v)
 \le\ell^{(n)}(h_{\bar p})
       -\frac12\int_0^1 B^{(n)}_{h_{\bar p}}(s)
                              (v(s)-h_{\bar p}(s))\,ds
       +\epsilon_n(H,A),
 \qquad \epsilon_n(H,A)\longrightarrow0.$$ The error is uniform over $\|v\|_\infty\le A$, the finite roundings, and their depths. Equal covariance levels simply repeat conditional products. Taking $\bar p\to p$ in <a href="#mag:block-tangent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mag:block-tangent">[mag:block-tangent]</a> and using <a href="#mag:block-consistency" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mag:block-consistency">[mag:block-consistency]</a> shows that the liminf cavity increment per spin is at least $$\label{mag:truncated-infimum}
 a_A-\epsilon_n(H,A),\qquad
 a_A=\inf_{p\in\mathcal P}\left\{G_1(p)+
       \sup_{\substack{v\in\mathcal H\\\|v\|_\infty\le A}}
          \left[\ell^{[\mathbf m]}(v)+\frac12\int_0^1p(s)v(s)\,ds\right]
                         \right\}.$$ This bound no longer depends on the limiting base array. The parameter sets supplied by the averaged perturbation estimates have probability tending to one. The uniform Jensen lower bound on the remaining increments, with $n$ fixed, allows parameter averaging; telescoping along every progression of step $n$ then gives the same lower pressure bound for the product of constrained blocks. Perturbations change its unnormalized expected log partition by $o(N)$, just as for the cube.

Restoring the field adds $\sum_j\gamma_jc_jm_j+o(1)$ per spin on these configurations. They form a subset of the original cube, so for each fixed $A$ we may first send $N\to\infty$ at fixed admissible $n$, with the cap and rounding limits already taken in the cavity calculation, and then send $n\to\infty$ along admissible multiples. Equation <a href="#mag:truncated-infimum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mag:truncated-infimum">[mag:truncated-infimum]</a> gives $$\liminf_{N\to\infty}\mathbb EP_N(c^{(N)})
        \ge\sum_j\gamma_jc_jm_j+a_A.$$

<a id="mag:height-limit"></a>
Finally, $$\label{mag:height-limit}
 a_A\uparrow\inf_{p\in\mathcal P}\{G_1(p)+S_{\mathbf m}(p)\}
                   \qquad(A\to\infty).$$ To justify the order of optimization, choose approximate minimizers $p_A$ along a sequence of heights tending to infinity. The set of nondecreasing functions valued in $[0,1]$ is compact in $L^1$, so a subsequence converges to $p$. The functional $G_1$ is continuous there. Every fixed finite-step field $v$ is eventually allowed in the truncated supremum, and $\int p_Av\to\int pv$. Thus the limiting truncated value is at least $G_1(p)+\ell^{[\mathbf m]}(v)+\tfrac12\int pv$ for every $v$. Taking the supremum proves the lower inequality in <a href="#mag:height-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mag:height-limit">[mag:height-limit]</a>; the reverse inequality follows from truncation. The values are finite above, for example by testing $p=0$, and bounded below by testing $v=0$. This completes the lower bound for each rational interior $\mathbf m$. Taking its supremum proves <a href="#mag:finite-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mag:finite-formula">[mag:finite-formula]</a> under the finite rational spectral restrictions.

We remove those restrictions before passing to general fields. The count comparison and the spectral bins in Section <a href="#sec:general" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:general">7</a> apply with the external field present: rotations within a repeated-eigenvalue group leave the interaction unchanged, so the same averaged projection bounds hold. On the formula side only $G_1$ depends on the spectral law, and its uniform difference is bounded by half the uniform difference of the corresponding $R$ functions. Hence all the infima and the outer supremum are stable under the spectral approximations. This proves the finite-field formula for the compact spectral law of the theorem. Likewise, finite field counts converging to $\gamma_j$ can be replaced, after a coordinate permutation, by periodic rational counts with bounded remainder and $o(N)$ changed sites. The mean absolute field change tends to zero. Haar permutation invariance identifies the expected pressures before and after the permutation.

<a id="mag:field-Lipschitz"></a>
For arbitrary deterministic vectors $c,d\in\mathbb R^N$, direct comparison of the exponents gives $$\label{mag:field-Lipschitz}
 |P_N(c)-P_N(d)|\le\frac1N\sum_{r=1}^N|c_r-d_r|.$$ For expected pressures we may permute coordinates first. On the line, matching sorted vectors realizes the minimum mean absolute difference, which is the $W_1$ distance between their equal-weight empirical laws. Applying <a href="#mag:field-Lipschitz" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mag:field-Lipschitz">[mag:field-Lipschitz]</a> to finite-field sequences and taking their proven limits shows that $\mathcal F_\mu$ is $1$-Lipschitz on finite-support laws with rational masses. Such laws are dense among probability laws of finite first moment: truncate the tails, use a finite continuity mesh, and approximate its masses by rationals. The Lipschitz bound proves existence and independence of the limit in <a href="#mag:W1-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mag:W1-formula">[mag:W1-formula]</a>. For a field sequence with empirical laws $\nu_N\to\nu$ in $W_1$, fix such an approximation $\nu_q$ and choose $N$-site field vectors with values in $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\nu_q$ and proportions converging to its masses. Their empirical laws then converge to $\nu_q$ in $W_1$. Matching the sorted vectors in <a href="#mag:field-Lipschitz" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mag:field-Lipschitz">[mag:field-Lipschitz]</a> and taking their proven finite-field limits gives $$\limsup_{N\to\infty}
 \bigl|\mathbb EP_N(c^{(N)})-\mathcal F_\mu(\nu_q)\bigr|
 \le W_1(\nu,\nu_q).$$ Letting $q\to\infty$ proves convergence of the expected pressure to $\mathcal F_\mu(\nu)$.

Finally, the Haar Lipschitz constant of the normalized pressure is independent of the deterministic external field. The fourth-moment concentration estimate of Lemma <a href="#rot:concentration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rot:concentration">14</a> and Borel–Cantelli therefore upgrade all these expected limits to almost-sure limits. This proves Theorem <a href="#mag:theorem" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="mag:theorem">34</a>.

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "The coupled cavity decomposition, with schematic block widths. The full and base interactions agree on the remaining spectral spaces $ℛ_{a}$. The span of the $m$ spectral images of the cavity coordinates splits into the special complement and the cavity itself; the full interaction on this span has each eigenvalue $λ_{a}$ with multiplicity $n$. Assigning $A_{0}$ to the special complement gives the prescribed base multiplicities. When $m = 1$, the special complement is absent. Conditional on the base spectral projectors, the $n − s_{a}$ special axes assigned to group $a$ form a uniform orthonormal frame. The base perturbations are generated independently of these frames conditional on those projectors.",
      "figure_id": "fig:cavity-decomposition",
      "pdf_page": 29,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:cavity-decomposition",
          "exact_quote": "The coupled cavity decomposition, with schematic block widths.\nThe full and base interactions agree on the remaining spectral spaces\n$\\mathcal R_a$. The span of the $m$ spectral images of the cavity\ncoordinates splits into the special compl",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[\n  x=1cm,y=1cm,\n  every node/.style={font=\\small,align=center,inner sep=2pt},\n  box/.style={draw=black!65,line width=.45pt},\n  retained/.style={box,fill=black!4},\n  special/.style={box,fill=blue!6},\n  cavity/.style={box,fill=orange!9}\n]\n  \\draw[black!65,line width=.45pt]\n    (6.85,5.30)--(6.85,5.45)--(15.50,5.45)--(15.50,5.30);\n  \\node[text width=8.3cm] at (11.175,6.15)\n    {$\\textstyle\\bigoplus_a\\operatorname{ran}(P_a^+E)$\\\\\n     $m$ spectral subspaces of dimension $n$};\n\n  \\draw[retained] (1.25,4.05) rectangle (6.15,5.10);\n  \\node at (3.70,4.575)\n    {$\\textstyle\\bigoplus_a\\mathcal R_a$\\\\dimension $N-d$};\n  \\draw[special] (6.85,4.05) rectangle (11.40,5.10);\n  \\node at (9.125,4.575)\n    {special complement $\\mathcal V_N$\\\\$d=(m-1)n$};\n  \\draw[cavity] (11.40,4.05) rectangle (15.50,5.10);\n  \\node at (13.45,4.575)\n    {cavity $E\\mathbb R^n$\\\\dimension $n$};\n\n  \\node[anchor=east] at (1.02,2.90) {Full};\n  \\draw[retained] (1.25,2.00) rectangle (6.15,3.80);\n  \\node at (3.70,2.90)\n    {$\\lambda_a$ on $\\mathcal R_a$\\\\[2pt]\n     $\\dim\\mathcal R_a=k_a(N+n)-n$};\n  \\node at (6.50,2.90) {$\\oplus$};\n  \\draw[special] (6.85,2.75) rectangle (11.40,3.80);\n  \\draw[box] (11.40,2.75) rectangle (15.50,3.80);\n  \\draw[box] (6.85,2.00) rectangle (11.40,2.75);\n  \\draw[cavity] (11.40,2.00) rectangle (15.50,2.75);\n  \\node at (9.125,3.275) {$A_N$};\n  \\node at (13.45,3.275) {$L_N$};\n  \\node at (9.125,2.375) {$L_N^{\\mathsf T}$};\n  \\node at (13.45,2.375) {$C_N$};\n\n  \\node[font=\\footnotesize,inner sep=.5pt] at (3.70,1.75) {retained without change};\n  \\node[anchor=east] at (1.02,.85) {Base};\n  \\draw[retained] (1.25,.20) rectangle (6.15,1.50);\n  \\node at (3.70,.85) {$\\lambda_a$ on $\\mathcal R_a$};\n  \\node at (6.50,.85) {$\\oplus$};\n  \\draw[special] (6.85,.20) rectangle (11.40,1.50);\n  \\node at (9.125,.85)\n    {$\\displaystyle A_0=\\bigoplus_a\\lambda_a I_{n-s_a}$};\n  \\node at (13.45,.85)\n    {adjoined spins\\\\$\\varepsilon\\in\\{-1,1\\}^n$};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A block diagram illustrating matrix/space decompositions with columns corresponding to dimensions and subspaces, and rows corresponding to \"Full\" and \"Base\" systems.\n- At the top spanning the right two columns: an overarching bracket labeled $\\bigoplus_a \\operatorname{ran}(P_a^+ E)$ and \"$m\\text{ spectral subspaces of dimension } n$\".\n- Three column header boxes below:\n  1. Left column: $\\bigoplus_a R_a$, \"dimension $N - d$\".\n  2. Middle column: \"special complement $V_N$\", \"$d = (m - 1)n$\".\n  3. Right column: \"cavity $E\\mathbb{R}^n$\", \"dimension $n$\".\n- Top row (labeled \"Full\"):\n  - Left box: \"$\\lambda_a\\text{ on } R_a$\", \"$\\dim R_a = k_a(N + n) - n$\".\n  - Followed by a direct sum symbol $\\oplus$.\n  - A $2 \\times 2$ block matrix partitioned into four blocks: top-left $A_N$, top-right $L_N$, bottom-left $L_N^T$, bottom-right $C_N$.\n- Text between the Full and Base rows below the left column box: \"retained without change\".\n- Bottom row (labeled \"Base\"):\n  - Left box: \"$\\lambda_a\\text{ on } R_a$\".\n  - Followed by a direct sum symbol $\\oplus$.\n  - Middle box: \"$A_0 = \\bigoplus_a \\lambda_a I_{n - s_a}$\".\n  - Right box: \"adjoined spins\", \"$\\varepsilon \\in \\{-1, 1\\}^n$\"."
    }
